for each irreducible non-trivial representation
\rho_{n}:\mathfrak{g}\rightarrow{\rm End}({{\mathbb{C}}}^{\tilde{N}_{n}})
. See Remark
7.2
.

When
n\in\mathbb{N}
is large, we see that the energy level is correspondingly large. By asymptotic freedom, the coupling constant
c
should weaken with large values of
n
. Since
c=1/\kappa
, this means that
\kappa
must be large when
n
is large. To define the momentum eigenvalues, we need to choose an unbounded sequence
\{\kappa_{n}:n\in\mathbb{N}\}
for Equation (
7.4
).

Recall we solved for the beta function in subsection
7.2
. By Definition
7.11
, we have that

\frac{\partial c}{\partial[\ln\tilde{N}]}=-\frac{c}{4}+\lambda(c),\ \ |\lambda(c)|\leq\tilde{C}_{4}c^{2}.

Now,

\displaystyle\frac{dc}{d[\ln\tilde{N}]}=-\frac{c}{4}+\lambda(c)\quad\Longrightarrow\frac{dc}{c-4\lambda(c)}=-\frac{d[\ln\tilde{N}]}{4}.

Write
\mu(c)=-4\lambda(c)/c
. Thus,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{c}\frac{dc}{1+\mu(c)}=-\frac{d[\ln\tilde{N}]}{4}\quad\Longrightarrow\left[\frac{1}{c}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}(-1)^{k}\mu(c)^{k}\right]dc=-\frac{d[\ln\tilde{N}]}{4},

whereby
\mu(c)
is such that
|\mu(c)|\leq\tilde{C}_{5}c
. Integrate, we obtain

\ln c+\tilde{\mu}(c)=-\frac{1}{4}\ln\tilde{N}+C,

C
is some constant. Note that
\tilde{\mu}(c)
is a scalar function such that
|\tilde{\mu}(c)|\leq\tilde{C}_{6}c
. Exponentiate, we have

ce^{\tilde{\mu}(c)}=\frac{1}{\hat{C}}\tilde{N}^{-1/4}\ \Longrightarrow\ c(1+\bar{\mu}(c))=\frac{1}{\hat{C}}\tilde{N}^{-1/4},

whereby
0<\hat{C}
is some positive constant and
\bar{\mu}(c)=e^{\tilde{\mu}(c)}-1
is such that
|\bar{\mu}(c)|\leq\tilde{C}_{7}c
. Thus, we have that
c\tilde{N}^{1/4}=\dfrac{1}{\hat{C}}\dfrac{1}{1+\bar{\mu}(c)}
and

\displaystyle\left|\frac{1}{\hat{C}}\frac{1}{1+\bar{\mu}(c)}\right|\leq\frac{1}{\hat{C}}\left[1+\tilde{C}_{8}|\bar{\mu}(c)|\right]\leq\frac{1}{\hat{C}}\left[1+\tilde{C}_{8}\tilde{C}_{7}c\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\hat{C}}\left[1+\tilde{C}_{7}\tilde{C}_{8}\frac{1}{\hat{C}}\tilde{N}^{-1/4}\left(1+\tilde{C}_{8}\tilde{C}_{7}\right)\right]=\frac{1}{\hat{C}}+\frac{\tilde{N}^{-1/4}}{\hat{C}^{2}}\tilde{C}_{7}\tilde{C}_{8}\left(1+\tilde{C}_{7}\tilde{C}_{8}\right).

Thus

\tilde{N}^{1/4}|\bar{\mu}(c)|\leq c\tilde{N}^{1/4}\tilde{C}_{7}\leq\frac{\tilde{C}_{7}}{\hat{C}}+\frac{\tilde{C}_{7}}{\hat{C}^{2}}\tilde{C}_{7}\tilde{C}_{8}\left(1+\tilde{C}_{7}\tilde{C}_{8}\right).

Recall
c=1/\kappa
. Hence,

\displaystyle\kappa=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{c}=\hat{C}\tilde{N}^{1/4}(1+\bar{\mu}(c))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\hat{C}\tilde{N}^{1/4}+\tilde{R}(c),

whereby the remainder term
\tilde{R}(c)
is such that

|\tilde{R}(c)|\leq\tilde{C}_{7}+\frac{\tilde{C}_{7}}{\hat{C}}\tilde{C}_{7}\tilde{C}_{8}\left(1+\tilde{C}_{7}\tilde{C}_{8}\right)=\tilde{C}_{7}+\frac{1}{\hat{C}}\tilde{C}_{9}.

In the above calculations,
\tilde{C}_{k}
are positive constants,
5\leq k\leq 9
, all independent of
\tilde{N}
. And we see that if
\hat{C}>1
, then we have that
|\tilde{R}(c)|\leq\tilde{C}_{7}+\tilde{C}_{9}
, independent of
\hat{C}
and
\tilde{N}
.

We will henceforth define for
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\kappa_{n}:=\hat{C}\tilde{N}_{n}^{1/4}+\hat{C}_{n}
, for some fixed positive constant
\hat{C}>1
and
|\hat{C}_{n}|\leq\tilde{C}_{7}+\tilde{C}_{9}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

Set
|a|=1
. Start with the representation
\rho_{1}:\mathfrak{g}\rightarrow{\rm End}({{\mathbb{C}}}^{\tilde{N}_{1}})
. We choose a constant
\hat{C}
large enough, such that when we plug in
\hat{C}\tilde{N}_{n}^{1/4}+\hat{C}_{1}=\kappa_{1}\geq\kappa_{0}
into Equation (
7.4
), we have

0<m_{1}^{2}:={{\rm{Tr}}}\ \epsilon(1,\kappa_{1})=\frac{\tilde{N}_{1}}{4}C_{2}(\rho_{1})+{{\rm{Tr}}}\ {{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\left(\cdot,\nu_{R[a]}^{\kappa_{1},\rho_{1}}\right)_{\sharp}\left(\cdot,\nu_{R_{\delta}[a]}^{\kappa_{1},\rho_{1}}\right)_{\sharp}\mathcal{Y}^{\kappa}\right],

{{\rm{Tr}}}
is a matrix trace. Since the mass gap
m_{1}>0
, we define the quantized momentum eigenvalue
\hat{P}(\rho_{1})^{2}:=\hat{H}(\rho_{1})^{2}-m_{1}^{2}
.

Take the trace on Equation (
7.4
),

-{{\rm{Tr}}}\ {{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\left(\cdot,\nu_{R[a]}^{\kappa,\rho_{n}}\right)_{\sharp}\left(\cdot,\nu_{R_{\delta}[a]}^{\kappa,\rho_{n}}\right)_{\sharp}\mathcal{Y}^{\kappa}\right]=\frac{\tilde{N}_{n}}{4}C_{2}(\rho_{n})-{{\rm{Tr}}}\ \epsilon(n,\kappa).

(7.10)

Rewrite this, we will obtain

\frac{4}{NC(\rho_{n})}{{\rm{Tr}}}\ {{\mathbb{E}}}\left[-\left(\cdot,\nu_{R[a]}^{\kappa,\rho_{n}}\right)_{\sharp}\left(\cdot,\nu_{R_{\delta}[a]}^{\kappa,\rho_{n}}\right)_{\sharp}\mathcal{Y}^{\kappa}\right]-1=-\frac{4{{\rm{Tr}}}\ \epsilon(n,\kappa)}{NC(\rho_{n})}.

Compare this with Equation (
7.9
). Note that we made use of Remark
7.2
. It remains to plug in
\kappa=\kappa_{n}
in the RHS of the equation, to determine the momentum eigenvalues.

Consider
n\geq 2
. For any irreducible representation
\rho_{n}:\mathfrak{g}\rightarrow{\rm End}({{\mathbb{C}}}^{\tilde{N}_{n}})
, we define
m_{n}
, such that

m_{n}^{2}:={{\rm{Tr}}}[\epsilon(n,\kappa_{n})].

(7.11)

From Equation (
7.5
), we have that for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
,

0<\frac{\underline{c}}{\kappa_{n}^{4}}<\frac{1}{C(\rho_{n})}{{\rm{Tr}}}[\epsilon(n,\kappa_{n})]\leq\frac{\bar{c}}{\kappa_{n}^{4}},

(7.12)

for positive constants
\underline{c},\bar{c}
independent of
\rho_{n}
.

Hence, the trace
{{\rm{Tr}}}[\epsilon(n,\kappa_{n})]
is of the order
C(\rho_{n})/\kappa_{n}^{4}
. In terms of the dimension scale, we see that it is of the order
C(\rho_{n})/\hat{C}^{4}\tilde{N}_{n}\equiv C_{2}(\rho_{n})/N\hat{C}^{4}
. From Equation (
7.12
), we must have that
m_{n}^{2}
is of the order
\dfrac{C_{2}(\rho_{n})}{\hat{C}^{4}}>0
, which tends to infinity, as
n\rightarrow\infty
, because
\{C_{2}(\rho_{n}):n\in\mathbb{N}\}
is unbounded,

Define the quantized momentum eigenvalue, in the direction of a unit vector
a
as

\displaystyle\hat{P}(\rho_{n})^{2}:=

\displaystyle-{{\rm{Tr}}}\ {{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\left(\cdot,\nu_{R[a]}^{\kappa_{n},\rho_{n}}\right)_{\sharp}\left(\cdot,\nu_{R_{\delta}[a]}^{\kappa_{n},\rho_{n}}\right)_{\sharp}\mathcal{Y}^{\kappa}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\tilde{N}_{n}}{4}C_{2}(\rho_{n})-{{\rm{Tr}}}\ \epsilon(n,\kappa_{n})=\frac{\tilde{N}_{n}}{4}C_{2}(\rho_{n})-m_{n}^{2}>0,

from Equations (
7.10
) and (
7.11
). Thus, the average of the flux through the time-like rectangular surface
R[a]
using the Yang-Mills measure, quantize the momentum in the direction
a
.

Note that
m_{n}^{2}/\hat{H}(\rho_{n})^{2}
, is of the order
\dfrac{1}{\tilde{N}_{n}\hat{C}^{4}}>0
. Hence, we see that
the momentum operator eigenvalues will go to infinity, from

\frac{\hat{P}(\rho_{n})^{2}}{\hat{H}(\rho_{n})^{2}}=1-\frac{m_{n}^{2}}{\hat{H}(\rho_{n})^{2}}\longrightarrow 1,

as
n\rightarrow\infty
.

Remark 7.12

Note that we interpret
\tilde{N}C_{2}(\rho)
as energy squared. Because of the beta function, we see that
\kappa
increases with the dimension of the irreducible representation. In the case of
{\rm SU}(2)
or
{\rm SU}(3)
, we see that the Casimir operator is large, when the dimension of the representation is large. More generally, the Weyl dimension formula says that when the dimension of the representation
\rho
is large, then
|\lambda_{\rho}|
will be large, which also implies that the Casimir constant
C_{2}(\rho)
will be large. Since the coupling constant
c=1/\kappa
, the coupling constant weakens at high energies, which the physicists will term as asymptotic freedom.

Definition 7.13

(Hamiltonian and momentum operator)

For each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, let
\rho_{n}
be an irreducible non-trivial representation for a simple Lie Algebra
\mathfrak{g}
. Recall we defined
\hat{H}(\rho_{n})^{2}=\frac{\tilde{N}_{n}}{4}C_{2}(\rho_{n})>0
and
\hat{P}(\rho_{n})
such that

\hat{H}(\rho_{n})^{2}-\hat{P}(\rho_{n})^{2}=m_{n}^{2}>0,

for some positive mass gap
m_{n}>0
defined by Equation (
7.11
).

From Definition
2.5
, we have a basis for
{{\mathbb{R}}}^{4}
. Define a Hamiltonian
\hat{H}(\vec{a},\rho)
, in the direction
\vec{a}\in{{\mathbb{R}}}^{4}
, as
\hat{H}(\vec{a},\rho):=(\vec{a}\cdot\hat{f}_{0})\hat{H}(\rho)\hat{f}_{0}
. Explicitly, if
\vec{a}=\sum_{b=0}^{3}a^{b}\hat{f}_{b}
, we will write
\hat{H}(\vec{a},\rho):=-a^{0}\hat{H}(\rho)\hat{f}_{0}
.

We define a momentum operator,
\hat{P}(\vec{a},\rho)
, in the direction
\vec{a}\in{{\mathbb{R}}}^{4}
, as
\hat{P}(\vec{a},\rho):=(\vec{a}\cdot\hat{f}_{1})\hat{P}(\rho)\hat{f}_{1}
. Explicitly, if
a=\sum_{b=0}^{3}a^{b}\hat{f}_{b}
, we will write
\hat{P}(\vec{a},\rho):=a^{1}\hat{P}(\rho)\hat{f}_{1}
. Thus

\displaystyle U(\vec{a},1)

\displaystyle\left(S,f_{\alpha}\otimes\rho(E^{\alpha}),\{\hat{f}_{a}\}_{a=0}^{3}\right)

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle e^{-i[\hat{f}_{0}\cdot\hat{H}(\vec{a},\rho)+\hat{f}_{1}\cdot\hat{P}(\vec{a},\rho)]}\left(S+\vec{a},f_{\alpha}(\cdot-\vec{a})\otimes\rho(E^{\alpha}),\{\hat{f}_{a}\}_{a=0}^{3}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e^{i[a^{0}\hat{H}(\rho)-a^{1}\hat{P}(\rho)]}\left(S+\vec{a},f_{\alpha}(\cdot-\vec{a})\otimes\rho(E^{\alpha}),\{\hat{f}_{a}\}_{a=0}^{3}\right).

We also define
\hat{H}(\rho_{0})=\hat{P}(\rho_{0})=0
, thus for the vacuum state,
\hat{P}1=\hat{H}1=0
. The momentum operator
\hat{P}
and Hamiltonian
\hat{H}
act on the Hilbert space
\mathbb{H}_{{\rm YM}}(\mathfrak{g})
, as

\displaystyle\hat{P}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}v_{n}:=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\hat{P}(\rho_{n})v_{n},\quad\hat{H}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}v_{n}:=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\hat{H}(\rho_{n})v_{n},

provided the sum converges. Because
\{C_{2}(\rho_{n})\}_{n\geq 1}
is unbounded and the eigenvalues are real, we see that
\hat{P}
and
\hat{H}
are unbounded self-adjoint operators.

Theorem 7.14

(Positive mass Gap Theorem)

Consider the Hilbert space defined in Equation (
2.1
). The quantized momentum operator
\hat{P}
and Hamiltonian
\hat{H}
are non-negative, unbounded self-adjoint operators on this Hilbert space. They annihilate the vacuum state 1 and their other eigenspaces are
\mathscr{H}(\rho_{n})
,
n\geq 1
, with corresponding eigenvalues
\hat{P}(\rho_{n})
and
\hat{H}(\rho_{n})
respectively, such that,
\hat{H}(\rho_{n})^{2}-\hat{P}(\rho_{n})^{2}=m_{n}^{2}
for some positive mass gap
m_{n}>0
.

These eigenvalues were chosen because of the Callan-Symanzik Equation, and we have that
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}m_{n}^{2}=\infty
. Thus
m_{0}:=\inf_{n\in\mathbb{N}}m_{n}>0
, showing the existence of a positive mass gap in a 4-dimensional quantum compact and simple Yang-Mills gauge theory.

Remark 7.15

In
[
21
]
, they explicitly state that the mass gap refers to the gap in the spectrum of the Hamiltonian operator. But this cannot be correct, as the mass gap refers to the difference between the Hamiltonian and the momentum operator. A strictly positive mass gap is required to show the short range nature of the weak force.

Our discussion on the construction of a 4-dimensional quantum Yang-Mills simple and compact gauge group is now complete. Does our construction apply to an abelian gauge group? Answer is no.

When we have an abelian group
{\rm U}(1)
, we can define a non-trivial representation
F_{a}:u\in\mathfrak{u}(1)\mapsto 0\neq a\in{{\mathbb{R}}}
, for any
a
. It is clear that
F_{a}
is a representation of
\mathfrak{u}(1)
. Essentially, there is only one irreducible representation of
\mathfrak{u}(1)
, up to a constant. As a consequence, using the construction as outlined, the momentum operator and Hamiltonian, will not be unbounded operators.

An abelian gauge group describes the quantum electromagnetic force. As photons are massless, we see that
(\hat{H},\hat{P},0,0)
must be a null-vector. As a result, the commutation and anti-commutation relations discussed in Section
5
will only hold on a 2-dimensional subspace.

A positive mass gap does not exist for an abelian gauge group
{\rm U}(1)
. The existence of a mass gap is due to the quartic term of the interaction term in the Yang-Mills action. And this gives us Equation (
7.4
), proved in
[
6
]
using asymptotic freedom and the structure constants of the Lie Algebra
\mathfrak{g}
, which do not apply to an abelian gauge group. Incidently, it will also give us the area law formula. Because of the absence of asymptotic freedom, the area law formula will not hold in the case of an abelian gauge group, as was shown in
[
8
]
.

8
Clustering

One important feature of the weak and strong force is that both are short range. This is formulated mathematically as the Clustering Theorem. To further validate that our construction of a 4-dimensional quantum field theory over in
{{\mathbb{R}}}^{4}
, satisfying Wightman’s axioms with a mass gap is correct, we will devote this section to the proof of the Clustering Theorem.

The first version was proved by Ruelle in
[
19
]
. The proof that it decays exponentially is taken from
[
22
]
. Our main reference for the Cluster Decomposition Property will be from
[
3
]
, and we will follow closely the notations used in there.

Remark 8.1

We will use the standard orthonormal basis
\{e_{a}\}_{a=0}^{3}
, whereby
\{e_{2},e_{3}\}
span
S_{0}
, and
\{e_{0},e_{1}\}
will span a time-like plane
S_{0}^{\flat}
. The coordinates used in this section will be pertaining to the standard orthonormal basis. Thus,
\vec{a}=(a^{0},a^{1},a^{2},a^{3})
means the vector
\sum_{b=0}^{3}a^{b}e_{b}\in{{\mathbb{R}}}^{4}
.

Definition 8.2

(Fourier Transform)

We define the Fourier Transform of a
L^{2}
function
f:{{\mathbb{R}}}\rightarrow{{\mathbb{R}}}
by

\hat{f}(p)=\mathcal{F}[f](p):=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{{{\mathbb{R}}}}e^{-ipx}f(x)\ dx.

On
{{\mathbb{R}}}^{4}
, our Fourier Transform is given by

\hat{f}(\vec{p})=\mathcal{F}[f](\vec{p}):=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{2}}\int_{{{\mathbb{R}}}^{4}}e^{-i\vec{p}\cdot\vec{x}}f(\vec{x})\ d\vec{x}.

See Equation (
8.1
).

Recall we fixed a surface
S_{0}
, which is the
x^{2}-x^{3}
plane, and we have the quantum state
\Big(S_{0},f_{\alpha}\otimes\rho(E^{\alpha}),\{e_{a}\}_{a=0}^{3}\Big)
. But how does one understand it? From the definition of the field operators in Section
3
, we see that
f_{\alpha}
is synonymous with
F_{\alpha}\left(\hat{H}(\rho),\hat{P}(\rho),x^{2},x^{3}\right)
, whereby
F_{\alpha}\left(\hat{H}(\rho),\hat{P}(\rho),\cdot,\cdot\right):S_{0}\rightarrow{{\mathbb{C}}}
, is a Schwartz function on
S_{0}
.

Let
\hat{F}_{\alpha}
be the Fourier Transform of
F_{\alpha}
. By taking the Fourier Transform of
F_{\alpha}
over in
S_{0}
,

\displaystyle\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\int_{S_{0}}e^{-i[q^{2}x^{2}+q^{3}x^{3}]}F_{\alpha}\left(\hat{H}(\rho),\hat{P}(\rho),x^{2},x^{3}\right)\ dx^{2}dx^{3}\otimes\rho(E^{\alpha})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\hat{F}_{\alpha}\left(\hat{H}(\rho),\hat{P}(\rho),q^{2},q^{3}\right)\otimes\rho(E^{\alpha}).

Hence one can view the quantum state as an operator-valued tempered distribution, over in momentum-energy space.

Remark 8.3

The momentum coordinates
\left(\hat{H}(\rho),\hat{P}(\rho),q^{2},q^{3}\right)
are with respect to the basis
\{e_{a}\}_{a=0}^{3}
. In general, if
S
is a space-like plane equipped with a Minkowski frame
\{\hat{f}_{a}\}_{a=0}^{3}
, then the Fourier Transform will yield
\hat{F}_{\alpha}\left(\hat{H}(\rho),\hat{P}(\rho),q^{2},q^{3}\right)\otimes\rho(E^{\alpha})
, but with the coordinates pertaining to the basis
\{\hat{f}_{a}\}_{a=0}^{3}
.

Thus, it is clear that
\hat{F}_{\alpha}
depends on the basis
\{\hat{f}_{a}\}_{a=0}^{3}
. We wish to point out that it is also dependent on
\vec{a}=a^{0}\hat{f}_{0}+a^{1}\hat{f}_{1}
in space-time, which is a position vector in the space-like plane
S
. This is because its Fourier Transform is

\displaystyle\hat{F}

\displaystyle\left(\hat{H}(\rho)\hat{f}_{0}+\hat{P}(\rho)\hat{f}_{1}+q^{2}\hat{f}_{2}+q^{3}\hat{f}_{3}\right)

\displaystyle:=\frac{e^{-i[a^{0}\hat{H}(\rho)-a^{1}\hat{P}(\rho)]}}{2\pi}\int_{\hat{s}\in{{\mathbb{R}}}^{2}}e^{-i(sq^{2}+\bar{s}q^{3})}f^{\{\hat{f}_{0},\hat{f}_{1}\}}(\hat{H}(\rho),\hat{P}(\rho))(s\hat{f}_{2}+\bar{s}\hat{f}_{3})\ d\hat{s}

(8.1)

\displaystyle\equiv e^{-i[a^{0}\hat{H}(\rho)-a^{1}\hat{P}(\rho)]}\hat{f}\left(\hat{H}(\rho)\hat{f}_{0}+\hat{P}(\rho)\hat{f}_{1}+q^{2}\hat{f}_{2}+q^{3}\hat{f}_{3}\right),

from Equation (
3.2
). Also refer to Item
3
in Remark
3.5
.

Hence,
\hat{F}_{\alpha}\left(\hat{H}(\rho)\hat{f}_{0}+\hat{P}(\rho)\hat{f}_{1}+q^{2}\hat{f}_{2}+q^{3}\hat{f}_{3}\right)\otimes\rho(E^{\alpha})
allows us to reconstruct

\left(S,f_{\alpha}\otimes\rho(E^{\alpha}),\{\hat{f}_{a}\}_{a=0}^{3}\right)
.

For a Schwartz function
f\in\mathscr{P}
,
{\rm supp}\ \hat{f}
may or may not lie in the positive light cone in energy-momentum space. Thus,
\hat{H}(\rho)^{2}-\hat{P}(\rho)^{2}-q^{2,2}-q^{2,3}
may not be greater than or equal to 0. But this does not matter, as we are still able to prove the Clustering Decomposition Property in Theorem
8.32
. Even though it is not required or necessary to impose the condition that
\hat{H}(\rho)^{2}-\hat{P}(\rho)^{2}-q^{2,2}-q^{2,3}\geq 0
, this train of thought is not quite correct.

In the proof of Clustering Theorem
8.34
, we will see that by a suitable choice of a Minkowski frame, we will instead use
\left(m,q^{1},q^{2},q^{3}\right)
as the coordinates in a 4-dimensional mass-momentum space after taking Fourier Transform. The translation operator in a time-like direction will be generated by mass, not energy. The momentum coordinates
q^{i}
’s will be free, and the existence of a positive mass gap
m_{0}
says that the total energy is given by
m^{2}+\sum_{i=1}^{3}|q^{i}|^{2}\geq m_{0}^{2}
.

Refer to Definition
7.13
. When we take the Fourier Transform of

U(\vec{a},1)\left(S_{0},f_{\alpha}\otimes\rho(E^{\alpha}),\{e_{a}\}_{a=0}^{3}\right)
, a similar set of calculations will give us

e^{-i\vec{a}\cdot\vec{\alpha}}e^{-i[q^{2}a^{2}+q^{3}a^{3}]}\hat{F}_{\alpha}\left(\hat{H}(\rho),\hat{P}(\rho),q^{2},q^{3}\right)\otimes\rho(E^{\alpha}),

\vec{\alpha}=(\hat{H}(\rho),\hat{P}(\rho),0,0)
.

Recall,
\{0\}\cup\{\hat{H}(\rho_{n}),\hat{P}(\rho_{n})\}_{n\geq 1}
are the discrete eigenvalues of the Hamiltonian and momentum operator. By the SNAG theorem, we see that when
\vec{a}\in S_{0}^{\flat}
, i.e.
\vec{a}=a^{0}e_{0}+a^{1}e_{1}
, the spectrum of the translation operator
U(\vec{a},1)
on
\mathbb{H}_{{\rm YM}}(\mathfrak{g})
is
\{1\}\cup\{e^{i[a^{0}\hat{H}(\rho_{n})-a^{1}\hat{P}(\rho_{n})]}\}_{n\geq 1}
, which is discrete.

Now, recall
S_{0}^{\flat}
is the
x^{0}-x^{1}
plane and we have the field operator
\phi^{\alpha,n}(f)
,
f\in\mathscr{P}
, defined in Definitions
3.7
and
3.12
. We can define a distribution, denoted as
\left\langle\phi^{\alpha,n}(\vec{x})1,\phi^{\beta,n}(\vec{y})1\right\rangle
, via sending
(f,g)\in\mathscr{P}\times\mathscr{P}
to

\left\langle\phi^{\alpha,n}(f)1,\phi^{\beta,n}(g)1\right\rangle:=C(\rho_{n}){{\rm{Tr}}}[-F^{\alpha}F^{\beta}]\int_{S_{0}}\left[f^{\{e_{0},e_{1}\}}\overline{g^{\{e_{0},e_{1}\}}}\right](\hat{H}(\rho_{n}),\hat{P}(\rho_{n}))(\hat{s})\ d\hat{s}.

From this, we can define an
\rho_{n}(\mathfrak{g})
-valued distribution
\phi^{\alpha,n}(\vec{x})
, and write

\phi^{\alpha,n}(f)1=\int_{\vec{x}\in{{\mathbb{R}}}^{4}}d\vec{x}\ f(\vec{x})\phi^{\alpha,n}(\vec{x})1,

when it acts on vacuum state. See
[
3
,
19
]
.

Using the transformation law for the field operator, we see that

U(\vec{a},1)\phi^{\alpha,n}(\vec{x})U(\vec{a},1)^{-1}=\phi^{\alpha,n}(\vec{x}+\vec{a}).

Remark 8.4

We present an alternative way to understand quantum fields. One will be tempted to view
\phi^{\alpha,n}(\vec{x})1
heuristically as

\frac{1}{2\pi}e^{i[x^{0}\hat{H}(\rho_{n})-x^{1}\hat{P}(\rho_{n})]}\delta(\cdot-(x^{2},x^{3}))\otimes\rho_{n}(F^{\alpha}),

whereby
\delta
is the 2-dimensional Dirac delta function. But this is not entirely correct.

Suppose we replace the Dirac delta function with a Gaussian function
p_{\kappa}^{\vec{x}}(\cdot)=(\kappa^{3}/2\sqrt{2\pi})\exp[-\kappa^{2}|\vec{x}-\cdot|^{2}/4]/(2\pi)
. When we take the inner product,

\int_{\vec{z}\in{{\mathbb{R}}}^{4}}p_{\kappa}^{\vec{x}}(\vec{z})p_{\kappa}^{\vec{y}}(\vec{z})\ d\vec{z}=\frac{\kappa^{2}}{4(2\pi)}\exp[-\kappa^{2}|\vec{x}-\vec{y}|^{2}/8].

Write
x^{+}=(0,0,x^{2},x^{3})
,
\hat{H}\equiv\hat{H}(\rho_{n})
and
\hat{P}\equiv\hat{P}(\rho_{n})
. Thus, we can approximate
\langle\phi^{\alpha,n}(\vec{x})1,\phi^{\beta,n}(\vec{y})1\rangle
with

\left\langle\frac{1}{2\pi}e^{i[x^{0}\hat{H}-x^{1}\hat{P}]}p_{\kappa}^{x^{+}}\otimes\rho_{n}(F^{\alpha}),\frac{1}{2\pi}e^{i[y^{0}\hat{H}-y^{1}\hat{P}]}p_{\kappa}^{y^{+}}\otimes\rho_{n}(F^{\beta})\right\rangle,

as

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{2}}

\displaystyle e^{i[\hat{H}(x^{0}-y^{0})-\hat{P}(x^{1}-y^{1})]}\int_{\vec{z}\in{{\mathbb{R}}}^{4}}p_{\kappa}^{x^{+}}(\vec{z})p_{\kappa}^{y^{+}}(\vec{z})\ d\vec{z}\cdot\left\langle\rho_{n}(F^{\alpha}),\rho_{n}(F^{\beta})\right\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\kappa^{2}}{4(2\pi)^{3}}e^{i[\hat{H}(x^{0}-y^{0})-\hat{P}(x^{1}-y^{1})]}\exp[-\kappa^{2}|x^{+}-y^{+}|^{2}/8]\cdot\left\langle\rho_{n}(F^{\alpha}),\rho_{n}(F^{\beta})\right\rangle.

Taking the limit, we see that we can understand the distribution
\left\langle\phi^{\alpha,n}(\vec{x})1,\phi^{\beta,n}(\vec{y})1\right\rangle
as

\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{2}}e^{i[\hat{H}(x^{0}-y^{0})-\hat{P}(x^{1}-y^{1})]}\cdot\delta(x^{+}-y^{+})\cdot C(\rho_{n}){{\rm{Tr}}}[-F^{\alpha}F^{\beta}],\ x^{+},y^{+}\in S_{0}.

Therefore, we can approximate
\langle\phi^{\alpha,n}(f)1,\phi^{\beta,n}(g)1\rangle
as

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{2}}

\displaystyle\int_{\vec{x},\vec{y}\in{{\mathbb{R}}}^{4}}f(\vec{x})\overline{g(\vec{y})}\left\langle e^{i(x^{0}\hat{H}-x^{1}\hat{P})}p_{\kappa}^{x^{+}}\otimes\rho_{n}(F^{\alpha}),e^{i(y^{0}\hat{H}-y^{1}\hat{P})}p_{\kappa}^{y^{+}}\otimes\rho_{n}(F^{\beta})\right\rangle d\vec{x}d\vec{y}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\kappa^{2}}{4}\int_{\hat{s},\hat{t}\in S_{0}}f^{\{e_{0},e_{1}\}}(\hat{s})\overline{g^{\{e_{0},e_{1}\}}}(\hat{t})\frac{1}{2\pi}\exp[-\kappa^{2}|\hat{s}-\hat{t}|^{2}/8]d\hat{s}d\hat{t}\cdot\left\langle\rho_{n}(F^{\alpha}),\rho_{n}(F^{\beta})\right\rangle,

which approaches to

\int_{\hat{s}\in S_{0}}[f^{\{e_{0},e_{1}\}}\overline{g^{\{e_{0},e_{1}\}}}](\hat{s})d\hat{s}\cdot\left\langle\rho_{n}(F^{\alpha}),\rho_{n}(F^{\beta})\right\rangle,

when
\kappa\rightarrow\infty
. This justifies the inner product given in Definition
2.9
. These calculations can also be found in
[
8
]
.

Observe that

\lim_{\kappa\rightarrow\infty}\int_{\vec{z}\in{{\mathbb{R}}}^{4}}p_{\kappa}^{\vec{x}}(\vec{z})f(\vec{z})\ d\vec{z}=0,

for any
f\in\mathscr{P}
. Thus,
\phi^{\alpha,n}(\vec{x})1
itself has no meaning. But, we can give meaning to
\int_{\vec{x}\in{{\mathbb{R}}}^{4}}d\vec{x}\ f(\vec{x})\phi^{\alpha,n}(\vec{x})1
.

8.1
Vacuum Expectation

Notation 8.5

Let
\{f_{1},\cdots,f_{r}\}
and
\{g_{1},\cdots,g_{s}\}
be two sets of compactly supported functions in
\mathscr{P}
. Let
\psi^{\alpha_{\tau},n}(f_{\tau})
be either the creation operator
\phi^{\alpha_{\tau},n}(f_{\tau})
or annihilation operator
\phi^{\alpha_{\tau},n}(f_{\tau})^{\ast}
. Similar notation for
\psi^{\beta_{\theta},n}(g_{\theta})
. Without loss of generality, we assume that
r\geq s
.

Fix a
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Throughout this subsection
8.1
, we will write
\hat{H}(\rho_{n})=\hat{H}
,
\hat{P}(\rho_{n})=\hat{P}
. Define

\displaystyle A_{r}^{n}=

\displaystyle\psi^{\alpha_{1},n}(f_{1})\cdots\psi^{\alpha_{r},n}(f_{r}),\quad B_{s}^{n}=\psi^{\beta_{1},n}(g_{1})\cdots\psi^{\beta_{s},n}(g_{s}).

Consider the vacuum expectation

\left\langle A_{r}^{n}P_{0}B_{s}^{n}1,1\right\rangle\equiv\left\langle A_{r}^{n}B_{s}^{n}1,1\right\rangle-\left\langle A_{r}^{n}1,1\right\rangle\left\langle B_{s}^{n}1,1\right\rangle.

Here,
P_{0}
is the orthogonal projection onto
\bigoplus_{n\geq 1}\mathscr{H}(\rho_{n})
.

To write down an explicit formula, we need the following notation.

Notation 8.6

Define
\left\{h_{\theta}\in\mathscr{P}\right\}_{\theta=1}^{r+s}
as follows. When
1\leq\theta\leq r
, then

h_{\theta}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}f_{\theta},&\hbox{{\rm if} $\psi^{\alpha_{\theta},n}(f_{\theta})=\phi^{\alpha_{\theta},n}(f_{\theta})$;}\\
-\overline{f}_{\theta},&\hbox{{\rm if} $\psi^{\alpha_{\theta},n}(f_{\theta})=\phi^{\alpha_{\theta},n}(f_{\theta})^{\ast}$.}\end{array}\right.

When
r+1\leq\theta\leq r+s
, then

h_{\theta}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}g_{\theta-r},&\hbox{{\rm if} $\psi^{\beta_{\theta-r},n}(g_{\theta-r})=\phi^{\beta_{\theta-r},n}(g_{\theta-r})$;}\\
-\overline{g}_{\theta-r},&\hbox{{\rm if} $\psi^{\beta_{\theta-r},n}(g_{\theta-r})=\phi^{\beta_{\theta-r},n}(g_{\theta-r})^{\ast}$.}\end{array}\right.

Similarly, define
\left\{\tilde{h}_{\theta}\in\mathscr{P}\right\}_{\theta=1}^{r+s}
as follows. When
1\leq\theta\leq s
, then

\tilde{h}_{\theta}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}g_{\theta},&\hbox{{\rm if} $\psi^{\alpha_{\theta},n}(g_{\theta})=\phi^{\alpha_{\theta},n}(g_{\theta})$;}\\
-\overline{g}_{\theta},&\hbox{{\rm if} $\psi^{\alpha_{\theta},n}(g_{\theta})=\phi^{\alpha_{\theta},n}(g_{\theta})^{\ast}$.}\end{array}\right.

When
s+1\leq\theta\leq r
, then

\tilde{h}_{\theta}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}f_{\theta-s},&\hbox{{\rm if} $\psi^{\alpha_{\theta},n}(f_{\theta-s})=\phi^{\alpha_{\theta},n}(f_{\theta-s})$;}\\
-\overline{f}_{\theta-s},&\hbox{{\rm if} $\psi^{\alpha_{\theta},n}(f_{\theta-s})=\phi^{\alpha_{\theta},n}(f_{\theta-s})^{\ast}$.}\end{array}\right.

When
r+1\leq\theta\leq r+s
, then

\tilde{h}_{\theta}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}f_{\theta-s},&\hbox{{\rm if} $\psi^{\beta_{\theta-r},n}(f_{\theta-s})=\phi^{\beta_{\theta-r},n}(f_{\theta-s})$;}\\
-\overline{f}_{\theta-s},&\hbox{{\rm if} $\psi^{\beta_{\theta-r},n}(f_{\theta-s})=\phi^{\beta_{\theta-r},n}(f_{\theta-s})^{\ast}$.}\end{array}\right.

For those
\theta\in\{1,2,\cdots,r+s\}
, such that

\displaystyle\psi^{\alpha_{\theta},n}(h)

\displaystyle=\phi^{\alpha_{\theta},n}(h)^{\ast},\ 1\leq\theta\leq r,

\displaystyle\psi^{\beta_{\theta-r},n}(h)

\displaystyle=\phi^{\beta_{\theta-r},n}(h)^{\ast},\ r+1\leq\theta\leq r+s,

we will say that
\theta
is adjoint. Then, we will write

\chi(\theta)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}-1,&\hbox{$\theta$ is adjoint;}\\
1,&\hbox{otherwise.}\end{array}\right.

For
\vec{x}=(x^{0},x^{1},x^{2},x^{3})
, we will write
d\vec{x}=dx^{0}dx^{1}dx^{2}dx^{3}
. And we will write
\vec{x}=(x^{-},x^{+})
,
x^{-}=(x^{0},x^{1})
,
x^{+}=(x^{2},x^{3})
,
dx^{-}=dx^{0}dx^{1}
,
dx^{+}=dx^{2}dx^{3}
.

A partition of
R=\{1,2,\cdots,r+s\}
is given by
Q=\{A_{1},\ldots,A_{n(Q)}\}
, whereby

•

R=\bigcup_{l=1}^{n(Q)}A_{l}
;

•

A_{l}\cap A_{\hat{l}}=\emptyset
, if
l\neq\hat{l}
;

•

A_{l}=\{z_{l},z_{l}+1,\cdots,z_{l}+k_{l}\}
, for some
z_{l}\in R
and some
0\leq k_{l}\leq r+s
.

Let
\Gamma
be the set of all such possible partitions of
R
. For a partition
Q=\{A_{1},\ldots,A_{n(Q)}\}\in\Gamma
, we will write

\displaystyle\int_{Q}\{h_{\theta}\}_{\theta=1}^{r+s}

\displaystyle:=\prod_{l=1}^{n(Q)}\left\{\int_{S_{0}}\left[\prod_{\theta\in A_{l}}\int_{y_{\theta}^{-}\in{{\mathbb{R}}}^{2}}\frac{e^{i\chi(\theta)[y_{\theta}^{0}\hat{H}-y_{\theta}^{1}\hat{P}]}}{2\pi}h_{\theta}(y_{\theta}^{-},y^{+})dy_{\theta}^{-}\right]dy^{+}\right\}

\displaystyle=\prod_{l=1}^{n(Q)}\left\{\int_{S_{0}}\left[\prod_{\theta\in A_{l}}\int_{y_{\theta}^{0},y_{\theta}^{1}\in{{\mathbb{R}}}}\frac{e^{i\chi(\theta)[y_{\theta}^{0}\hat{H}-y_{\theta}^{1}\hat{P}]}}{2\pi}h_{\theta}(y_{\theta}^{0},y_{\theta}^{1},y^{2},y^{3})dy_{\theta}^{0}dy_{\theta}^{1}\right]dy^{2}dy^{3}\right\}.

Note that it is a product of
n(Q)
integrals and
A_{l}=\{z,z+1,\cdots,z+k_{l}\}
for some
k_{l}
.

By abuse of notation, we will write
R=\{1,2,\cdots,r+s\}\in\Gamma
, which is itself a partition of
R
.

From Definitions
3.7
,
3.12
,
3.14
, we see that we can write

\displaystyle\left\langle A_{r}^{n}P_{0}B_{s}^{n}1,1\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{Q\in\Gamma}c_{Q}\int_{Q}\{h_{\theta}\}_{\theta=1}^{r+s},

(8.2)

for some set of complex coefficients
\{c_{Q}\in{{\mathbb{C}}}\}_{Q\in\Gamma}
.

Remark 8.7

It is not difficult to see from Definitions
3.12
and
3.14
, that if
Q
contains a subset
\{z\}
,
z\in R
, then we must have
c_{Q}=0
.

Definition 8.8

Refer to Notation
8.5
. Recall we assume
r\geq s
. Define

\displaystyle C_{r}^{n}

\displaystyle=\psi^{\alpha_{1},n}(g_{1})\cdots\psi^{\alpha_{s},n}(g_{s})\psi^{\alpha_{s+1},n}(f_{1})\cdots\psi^{\alpha_{r},n}(f_{r-s}),

\displaystyle D_{s}^{n}

\displaystyle=\psi^{\beta_{1},n}(f_{r-s+1})\cdots\psi^{\beta_{s},n}(f_{r}).

Refer to Notation
8.6
.
Define the following tempered distribution

\displaystyle\mathscr{W}^{n}

\displaystyle:f_{1}\otimes_{{\mathbb{R}}}\cdots\otimes_{{\mathbb{R}}}f_{r}\otimes_{{\mathbb{R}}}g_{1}\otimes_{{\mathbb{R}}}\cdots\otimes_{{\mathbb{R}}}g_{s}\longmapsto\left\langle A_{r}^{n}P_{0}B_{s}^{n}1,1\right\rangle.

Here,

\displaystyle\mathscr{W}^{n}\left(\{\vec{x}_{\tau}\}_{\tau=1}^{r},\{\vec{x}_{\theta}\}_{\theta=r+1}^{r+s}\right)

\displaystyle\equiv\mathscr{W}^{n}\left(\{\vec{x}_{\tau}\}_{\tau=1}^{s},\{\vec{x}_{\theta}\}_{\theta=s+1}^{s+r}\right)

\displaystyle:=\mathscr{W}^{n}\left(\vec{x}_{1},\cdots,\vec{x}_{r},\vec{x}_{r+1},\cdots,\vec{x}_{r+s}\right),

is a tempered distribution such that (
\vec{x}=(x^{0},x)=(x^{0},x^{1},x^{2},x^{3})\in{{\mathbb{R}}}^{4}
)

\displaystyle\left\langle A_{r}^{n}B_{s}^{n}1,1\right\rangle-\left\langle A_{r}^{n}1,1\right\rangle\left\langle B_{s}^{n}1,1\right\rangle\equiv\left\langle A_{r}^{n}P_{0}B_{s}^{n}1,1\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\int_{{{\mathbb{R}}}^{4}\times\cdots\times{{\mathbb{R}}}^{4}}\mathscr{W}^{n}\left(\{\vec{x}_{\tau}\}_{\tau=1}^{r},\{\vec{x}_{\theta}\}_{\theta=r+1}^{r+s}\right)\bigotimes_{\tau=1}^{r+s}p_{\tau}(\vec{x}_{\tau})\cdot\prod_{\tau=1}^{r+s}d\vec{x}_{\tau}

\displaystyle=c_{R}\int_{S_{0}}\left[\prod_{\tau=1}^{r}\int_{x_{\tau}^{-}\in{{\mathbb{R}}}^{2}}E(x_{\tau}^{-})h_{\tau}(x_{\tau}^{-},x^{+})dx_{\tau}^{-}\cdot\prod_{\theta=r+1}^{r+s}\int_{x_{\theta}^{-}\in{{\mathbb{R}}}^{2}}E(x_{\theta}^{-})h_{\theta}(x_{\theta}^{-},x^{+})\ dx_{\theta}^{-}\right]dx^{+}

\displaystyle+\sum_{\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{Q \neq R}{Q \in\Gamma}}c_{Q}\int_{Q}\{h_{\theta}\}_{\theta=1}^{r+s},

(8.3)

whereby
E(x_{\tau}^{-})=E(x_{\tau}^{0},x_{\tau}^{1})=\dfrac{e^{i\chi(\tau)[x_{\tau}^{0}\hat{H}-x_{\tau}^{1}\hat{P}]}}{2\pi}
.
Note that
\chi(\tau)
was defined in Notation
8.6
and
p_{\tau}=f_{\tau}
if
1\leq\tau\leq r
;
p_{\tau}=g_{\tau-r}
if
r+1\leq\tau\leq r+s
.

If we switch the sets such that

\mathscr{W}^{n}:g_{1}\otimes_{{\mathbb{R}}}\cdots\otimes_{{\mathbb{R}}}g_{s}\otimes_{{\mathbb{R}}}f_{1}\otimes_{{\mathbb{R}}}\cdots\otimes_{{\mathbb{R}}}f_{r}\longmapsto\left\langle C_{r}^{n}P_{0}D_{s}^{n}1,1\right\rangle,

then

\displaystyle\left\langle C_{r}^{n}D_{s}^{n}1,1\right\rangle-\left\langle C_{r}^{n}1,1\right\rangle\left\langle D_{s}^{n}1,1\right\rangle\equiv\left\langle C_{r}^{n}P_{0}D_{s}^{n}1,1\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\int_{{{\mathbb{R}}}^{4}\times\cdots\times{{\mathbb{R}}}^{4}}\mathscr{W}^{n}\left(\{\vec{x}_{\tau}\}_{\tau=1}^{s},\{\vec{x}_{\theta}\}_{\theta=s+1}^{s+r}\right)\bigotimes_{\tau=1}^{s+r}\tilde{p}_{\tau}(\vec{x}_{\tau})\cdot\prod_{\tau=1}^{s+r}d\vec{x}_{\tau}

\displaystyle=c_{R}\int_{S_{0}}\left[\prod_{\tau=1}^{s}\int_{x_{\tau}^{-}\in{{\mathbb{R}}}^{2}}E(x_{\tau}^{-})\tilde{h}_{\tau}(x_{\tau}^{-},x^{+})dx_{\tau}^{-}\cdot\prod_{\theta=s+1}^{s+r}\int_{x_{\theta}^{-}\in{{\mathbb{R}}}^{2}}E(x_{\theta}^{-})\tilde{h}_{\theta}(x_{\theta}^{-},x^{+})\ dx_{\theta}^{-}\right]dx^{+}

\displaystyle+\sum_{\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{Q \neq R}{Q \in\Gamma}}c_{Q}\int_{Q}\{\tilde{h}_{\theta}\}_{\theta=1}^{s+r}.

(8.4)

Note that
\tilde{p}_{\tau}=g_{\tau}
if
1\leq\tau\leq s
;
\tilde{p}_{\tau}=f_{\tau-s}
if
s+1\leq\tau\leq s+r
.

Remark 8.9

Refer to Remark
8.22
.

Write
B_{s}^{n,\vec{a}}1
as

\displaystyle\psi^{\beta_{1},n}(g_{1}(\cdot-\vec{a}))\cdots\psi^{\beta_{s-1},n}(g_{s-1}(\cdot-\vec{a}))\Big(S_{0}+\vec{a},g_{s}^{\{e_{0},e_{1}\}}(\cdot-\vec{a})\otimes\rho_{n}(F^{\beta_{s}}),\{e_{a}\}_{a=0}^{3}\Big),

and
\psi^{\beta_{\tau},n}(g_{\tau})_{U(\vec{a})}:=U(\vec{a},1)\psi^{\beta_{\tau},n}(g_{\tau})U(\vec{a},1)^{-1}
, for
1\leq\tau\leq s
.

By definition of the field operator and its adjoint, we have from Proposition
4.1
,

\displaystyle P_{0}U(\vec{a},1)B_{s}^{n}1=

\displaystyle P_{0}\psi^{\beta_{1},n}(g_{1})_{U(\vec{a})}\psi^{\beta_{2},n}(g_{2})_{U(\vec{a})}\cdots\psi^{\beta_{s-1},n}(g_{s-1})_{U(\vec{a})}U(\vec{a},1)\psi^{\beta_{s},n}(g_{s})1

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e^{i[a^{0}\hat{H}-a^{1}\hat{P}]}P_{0}B_{s}^{n,\vec{a}}1=e^{i[a^{0}\hat{H}-a^{1}\hat{P}]}\left[B_{s}^{n,\vec{a}}1-\left\langle B_{s}^{n}1,1\right\rangle 1\right],

for
\vec{a}=\sum_{b=0}^{3}a^{b}e_{b}
.

Hence, we have

\left\langle A_{r}^{n}P_{0}U(\vec{a},1)B_{s}^{n}1,1\right\rangle=e^{i[a^{0}\hat{H}-a^{1}\hat{P}]}\left[\left\langle A_{r}^{n}B_{s}^{n,\vec{a}}1,1\right\rangle-\left\langle A_{r}^{n}1,1\right\rangle\left\langle B_{s}^{n}1,1\right\rangle\right].

(8.5)

When
\vec{a}\notin S_{0}
, by our construction, we see that
\left\langle A_{r}^{n}P_{0}U(\vec{a},1)B_{s}^{n}1,1\right\rangle
is zero, because
S_{0}\cap(S_{0}+\vec{a})=\emptyset
.

By definition, we have

\displaystyle e^{i[a^{0}\hat{H}-a^{1}\hat{P}]}

\displaystyle\int_{{{\mathbb{R}}}^{2}}\frac{e^{i[s\hat{H}-t\hat{P}]}}{2\pi}f\left(s,t,x^{2},x^{3}\right)\ dsdt

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{{{\mathbb{R}}}^{2}}\frac{e^{i[(s+a^{0})\hat{H}-(t+a^{1})\hat{P}]}}{2\pi}f\left(s,t,x^{2},x^{3}\right)\ dsdt

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{{{\mathbb{R}}}^{2}}\frac{e^{i[s\hat{H}-t\hat{P}]}}{2\pi}f\left(s-a^{0},t-a^{1},x^{2},x^{3}\right)\ dsdt

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle f\left(\cdot-(a^{0},a^{1},0,0)\right)^{\{e_{0},e_{1}\}}(\hat{H},\hat{P})(0,0,x^{2},x^{3}).

(8.6)

Hence, after a translation by
\vec{a}=(a^{0},a^{1},0,0)
, we can consider

e^{i[a^{0}\hat{H}-a^{1}\hat{P}]}\left(S_{0}+\vec{a},f^{\{e_{0},e_{1}\}}(\cdot-\vec{a})\otimes\rho(F^{\alpha}),\{e_{0},e_{1}\}\right)
, as

\left(S_{0},f(\cdot-\vec{a})^{\{e_{0},e_{1}\}}\otimes\rho(F^{\alpha}),\{e_{a}\}_{a=0}^{3}\right)
. Thus, we will define the following.

Notation 8.10

Let
\vec{a}=(a^{0},a)\in{{\mathbb{R}}}^{4}
. Write

\mathscr{W}^{n}\left(\{\vec{x}_{\tau}\}_{\tau=1}^{r},\{\vec{x}_{\theta}+\vec{a}\}_{\theta=r+1}^{r+s}\right):=\mathscr{W}^{n}(\vec{x}_{1},\cdots,\vec{x}_{r},\vec{x}_{r+1}+\vec{a},\cdots,\vec{x}_{r+s}+\vec{a}).

And write
h_{\theta}^{\vec{a}}(\vec{y})=h_{\theta}(y^{0},y)
if
1\leq\theta\leq r
;
h_{\theta}^{\vec{a}}(\vec{y})=h_{\theta}(y^{0}-a^{0},y-a)
if
r+1\leq\theta\leq r+s
.

Definition 8.11

Refer to Definition
7.13
, Notations
8.6
and
8.10
.
Write
\vec{a}=(a^{0},a)=(a^{0},a^{1},a^{2},a^{3})\in{{\mathbb{R}}}^{4}
.

Using Equations (
8.2
), (
8.5
) and (
8.6
), we will define

\displaystyle H^{n}(\vec{a})

\displaystyle:=\int_{{{\mathbb{R}}}^{4}\times\cdots\times{{\mathbb{R}}}^{4}}\mathscr{W}^{n}\left(\{\vec{x}_{\tau}\}_{\tau=1}^{r},\{\vec{x}_{\theta}\}_{\theta=r+1}^{r+s}\right)\bigotimes_{\tau=1}^{r}p_{\tau}(\vec{x}_{\tau})\cdot\bigotimes_{\theta=r+1}^{r+s}p_{\theta}(\vec{x}_{r+\theta}-\vec{a})\cdot\prod_{\tau=1}^{r+s}d\vec{x}_{\tau}

\displaystyle\equiv\int_{{{\mathbb{R}}}^{4}\times\cdots\times{{\mathbb{R}}}^{4}}\mathscr{W}^{n}\left(\{\vec{x}_{\tau}\}_{\tau=1}^{r},\{\vec{x}_{\theta}+\vec{a}\}_{\theta=r+1}^{r+s}\right)\bigotimes_{\tau=1}^{r+s}p_{\tau}(\vec{x}_{\tau})\cdot\prod_{\tau=1}^{r+s}d\vec{x}_{\tau}

\displaystyle=c_{R}\int_{S_{0}}\left[\prod_{\tau=1}^{r}\int_{x_{\tau}^{-}\in{{\mathbb{R}}}^{2}}E(x_{\tau}^{-})h_{\tau}(x_{\tau}^{-},x^{+})dx_{\tau}^{-}\cdot\prod_{\theta=r^{+}}^{r+s}\int_{x_{\theta}^{-}\in{{\mathbb{R}}}^{2}}E(x_{\theta}^{-})h_{\theta}^{\vec{a}}(x_{\theta}^{-},x^{+})dx_{\theta}^{-}\right]\ dx^{+}

\displaystyle+\sum_{\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{Q \neq R}{Q \in\Gamma}}c_{Q}\int_{Q}\{h_{\theta}^{\vec{a}}\}_{\theta=1}^{r+s},

(8.7)

where
E(x_{\tau}^{-})=E(x_{\tau}^{0},x_{\tau}^{1})=\dfrac{e^{i\chi(\tau)[x_{\tau}^{0}\hat{H}-x_{\tau}^{1}\hat{P}]}}{2\pi}
and
p_{\tau}=f_{\tau}
if
1\leq\tau\leq r
;
p_{\tau}=g_{\tau-r}
if
r+1\leq\tau\leq r+s
.

Remark 8.12

We will use
H^{n}(\vec{a})
, instead of
\left\langle A_{r}^{n}P_{0}U(\vec{a},1)B_{s}^{n}1,1\right\rangle
, to prove both the Cluster Decomposition Property and Clustering Theorem. When
\vec{a}\in S_{0}
, then they are equal to each other.

Lemma 8.13

Refer to Notation
8.5
, whereby we have the two sets
\{\psi^{\alpha_{\tau},n}\}_{\tau=1}^{r}
and
\{\psi^{\beta_{\theta},n}\}_{\theta=1}^{s}
. Suppose
the sum
\sum_{\tau=1}^{r}\chi(\tau)+\sum_{\theta=r+1}^{r+s}\chi(\theta)=0
,
\chi
was defined in Notation
8.6
.

Define for
1\leq i\leq r+s-1
,
\vec{\xi}_{i}=\vec{x}_{i}-\vec{x}_{i+1}
, which are relative coordinates. Let
\vec{a}=(a^{0},a^{1},a^{2},a^{3})
, pertaining to the standard orthonormal basis.

1.

For any
1\leq\alpha\leq r+s
,
\mathscr{W}^{n}
is independent of the variables
\{x_{\alpha}^{2},x_{\alpha}^{3}\}
.

2.

There exists a tempered distribution
W^{n}
, such that

\mathscr{W}^{n}\left(\{\vec{x}_{\tau}\}_{\tau=1}^{r},\{\vec{x}_{\theta}+\vec{a}\}_{\theta=r+1}^{r+s}\right)=W^{n}\left(\vec{\xi}_{1},\cdots,\vec{\xi}_{r-1},\vec{\xi}_{r}-\vec{a},\vec{\xi}_{r+1},\cdots,\vec{\xi}_{r+s-1}\right).

3.

Their Fourier Transforms are related by

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathscr{W}}^{n}

\displaystyle\left(\vec{p}_{1},\cdots,\vec{p}_{r+s}\right)

\displaystyle=(2\pi)^{4}\delta\left(\sum_{\tau=1}^{r+s}\vec{p}_{\tau}\right)\widehat{W}^{n}\left(\vec{p}_{1},\vec{p}_{1}+\vec{p}_{2},\cdots,\vec{p}_{1}+\vec{p}_{2}+\cdots+\vec{p}_{r+s-1}\right).

(8.8)

Proof.
Recall we have a set of Schwartz functions
\{f_{1},\cdots,f_{r},g_{1},\cdots g_{s}\}\subset\mathscr{P}
. Since
\sum_{\tau=1}^{r}\chi(\tau)+\sum_{\theta=r+1}^{r+s}\chi(\theta)=0
, the set of adjoint integers
\{\delta_{1},\cdots,\delta_{c}\}
is in one to one correspondence with the set of non-adjoint integers
\{\epsilon_{1},\cdots,\epsilon_{c}\}
. We will pair each
\delta_{i}
with
\epsilon_{i}
. Note that for each
i=1,\cdots,c
,
c=\frac{r+s}{2}
, we can write

\vec{x}_{\delta_{i}}-\vec{x}_{\epsilon_{i}}=\vec{\xi}_{\delta_{i}}+\vec{\xi}_{\delta_{i}+1}+\cdots+\vec{\xi}_{\epsilon_{i}-1},

(8.9)

when
\delta_{i}<\epsilon_{i}
. When
\delta_{i}>\epsilon_{i}
, we have

\vec{x}_{\delta_{i}}-\vec{x}_{\epsilon_{i}}=-\left(\vec{\xi}_{\epsilon_{i}}+\vec{\xi}_{\epsilon_{i}+1}+\cdots+\vec{\xi}_{\delta_{i}-1}\right).

(8.10)

Hence, we can write

\sum_{\tau=1}^{r}\chi(\tau)[x_{\tau}^{0}\hat{H}-x_{\tau}^{1}\hat{P}]+\sum_{\theta=r+1}^{r+s}\chi(\theta)[x_{\theta}^{0}\hat{H}-x_{\theta}^{1}\hat{P}]=-\sum_{i=1}^{r+s-1}c_{i}[\xi_{i}^{0}\hat{H}-\xi_{i}^{1}\hat{P}],

(8.11)

for some integers
c_{i}
’s. Therefore,

\prod_{\tau=1}^{r}E(x_{\tau}^{-})\prod_{\theta=r+1}^{r+s}E(x_{\theta}^{-})=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{r+s}}\exp\left[-i\sum_{j=1}^{r+s-1}c_{j}[\xi_{j}^{0}\hat{H}-\xi_{j}^{1}\hat{P}]\right].

(8.12)

Thus, Remark
8.7
and Equation (
8.7
) imply that

1.

\mathscr{W}^{n}
is independent of any chosen variables
\{x_{\alpha}^{2},x_{\alpha}^{3}\}
, by letting
x^{+}=(x_{\alpha}^{2},x_{\alpha}^{3})
in the equation;

2.

there is a tempered distribution
W^{n}
such that

W^{n}\left(\vec{\xi}_{1},\cdots,\vec{\xi}_{r+s-1}\right):=\mathscr{W}^{n}\left(\{\vec{x}_{\tau}\}_{\tau=1}^{r},\{\vec{x}_{\theta}\}_{\theta=r+1}^{r+s}\right).

We also have

\mathscr{W}^{n}\left(\{\vec{x}_{\tau}\}_{\tau=1}^{r},\{\vec{x}_{\theta}+\vec{a}\}_{\theta=r+1}^{r+s}\right)=W^{n}\left(\vec{\xi}_{1},\cdots,\vec{\xi}_{r-1},\vec{\xi}_{r}-\vec{a},\vec{\xi}_{r+1},\cdots,\vec{\xi}_{r+s-1}\right),

by definition of
\vec{\xi}_{j}
’s.

The proof for Equation (
8.8
) can be found in
[
3
]
, hence omitted.

Lemma 8.14

Refer to Definition
7.13
. Assume the sum
\sum_{\tau=1}^{r}\chi(\tau)+\sum_{\theta=r+1}^{r+s}\chi(\theta)=0
. Recall in the proof of Lemma
8.13
, we defined the integers
c_{i}
’s in Equation (
8.11
). For
\vec{a}\in{{\mathbb{R}}}^{4}
, write
a^{-}=(a^{0},a^{1},0,0)
,
a^{+}=(0,0,a^{2},a^{3})
,
da^{-}=da^{0}da^{1}
, and
da^{+}=da^{2}da^{3}
. For any
1\leq t\leq r+s-1
, we have

\int_{{{\mathbb{R}}}^{2}}e^{-ia^{-}\cdot q^{-}}W^{n}\left(\vec{\xi}_{1},\cdots,\vec{\xi}_{t}-a^{-},\vec{\xi}_{t+1},\cdots,\vec{\xi}_{r+s-1}\right)\ da^{-}

vanishes if
q^{-}\neq-c_{t}(\hat{H}(\rho_{n}),\hat{P}(\rho_{n}),0,0)
.

Proof.
Let
\vec{\alpha}=(\hat{H}(\rho_{n}),\hat{P}(\rho_{n}),0,0)
, and
t^{+}\equiv t+1
. Using Equations (
8.7
) and (
8.12
), we see that

W^{n}\left(\vec{\xi}_{1},\cdots,\vec{\xi}_{t}-a^{-},\vec{\xi}_{t+1},\cdots,\vec{\xi}_{r+s-1}\right)=e^{-ic_{t}a^{-}\cdot\vec{\alpha}}W^{n}\left(\vec{\xi}_{1},\cdots,\vec{\xi}_{t},\vec{\xi}_{t+1},\cdots,\vec{\xi}_{r+s-1}\right).

(8.13)

If we take the Fourier Transform,

\displaystyle\int_{{{\mathbb{R}}}^{2}}

\displaystyle e^{-iq^{-}\cdot a^{-}}W^{n}\left(\vec{\xi}_{1},\cdots,\vec{\xi}_{t}-a^{-},\vec{\xi}_{t^{+}},\cdots,\vec{\xi}_{r+s-1}\right)\ da^{-}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{{{\mathbb{R}}}^{2}}e^{-ia^{-}\cdot q^{-}}e^{-ic_{t}a^{-}\cdot\vec{\alpha}}W^{n}\left(\vec{\xi}_{1},\cdots,\vec{\xi}_{t},\vec{\xi}_{t^{+}},\cdots,\vec{\xi}_{r+s-1}\right)\ da^{-}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\pi W^{n}\left(\vec{\xi}_{1},\cdots,\vec{\xi}_{t},\cdots,\vec{\xi}_{r+s-1}\right)\cdot\delta\left(q^{-}+c_{t}(\hat{H}(\rho_{n}),\hat{P}(\rho_{n}),0,0)\right).

This completes the proof.

Remark 8.15

If one examines the proof, we note that the Fourier Transform of

a^{-}\longmapsto F^{-}(a^{-})\equiv W^{n}\left(\vec{\xi}_{1},\cdots,\vec{\xi}_{r}-a^{-},\vec{\xi}_{r+1},\cdots,\vec{\xi}_{r+s-1}\right),

is simply a multiple of the Dirac delta function
\delta\left(\lambda_{r}^{-}+c_{r}(\hat{H}(\rho_{n}),\hat{P}(\rho_{n}))\right)
. From Equation (
8.8
),
\lambda_{r}^{-}=\sum_{i=1}^{r}p_{i}^{-}
,
p_{i}^{-}\equiv(p_{i}^{0},p_{i}^{1})
, is the momenta variable conjugate to position variable
x_{i}^{-}\equiv(x_{i}^{0},x_{i}^{1})
.

A similar calculation will show that the Fourier Transform of

\displaystyle a^{-}\longmapsto F^{+}(a^{-})

\displaystyle=\mathscr{W}^{n}\left(\{\vec{x}_{\theta}+a^{-}\}_{\theta=1}^{s},\{\vec{x}_{\tau}\}_{\tau=s+1}^{s+r}\right)

\displaystyle\equiv W^{n}\left(\vec{\xi}_{1},\cdots,\vec{\xi}_{s}+a^{-},\vec{\xi}_{s+1},\cdots,\vec{\xi}_{r+s-1}\right),

is simply a multiple of the Dirac delta function
\delta\left(\lambda_{s}^{-}-c_{s}(\hat{H}(\rho_{n}),\hat{P}(\rho_{n}))\right)
. In this case, the support is on the set
\left\{\lambda_{s}^{-}=c_{s}[\hat{H}(\rho_{n})e_{0}+\hat{P}(\rho_{n})e_{1}]\right\}
,
c_{s}\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Definition 8.16

Refer to Notation
8.5
. We say that the set
\{\psi^{\alpha_{\tau},n}\}_{\tau=1}^{r}
is regular if there exists a unique
0\leq k\leq r
such that for any
h\in\mathscr{P}
,

•

\psi^{\alpha_{\tau},n}(h)=\phi^{\alpha_{\tau},n}(h)^{\ast}
, for every
1\leq\tau\leq k
, and

•

\psi^{\alpha_{\tau},n}(h)=\phi^{\alpha_{\tau},n}(h)
, for every
k+1\leq\tau\leq r
.

Proposition 8.17

Refer to Notation
8.5
, whereby we have the two sets
\{\psi^{\alpha_{\tau},n}\}_{\tau=1}^{r}
and
\{\psi^{\beta_{\theta},n}\}_{\theta=1}^{s}
,
r\geq s
. Suppose

•

both sets are regular as defined in Definition
8.16
;

•

the former has
0\neq\bar{k}
annihilation operators, and
\bar{l}:=r-\bar{k}
creation operators;

•

the latter has
\underline{k}
annihilation operators, and
0\neq\underline{l}:=s-\underline{k}
creation operators;

•

the sum
\sum_{\tau=1}^{r}\chi(\tau)+\sum_{\theta=r+1}^{r+s}\chi(\theta)=0
,
\chi
was defined in Notation
8.6
. That is, we have equal number of annihilation and creation operators, i.e.

\bar{k}+\underline{k}=\bar{l}+\underline{l}.

(8.14)

Suppose
{\rm min}\ \{\underline{k},\bar{l}\}\neq 0
. If
\bar{k}>\bar{l}
, then the coefficients
c_{i}
’s in Equation (
8.11
) can all be chosen to be positive. In particular, we have that
c_{r}>0
.
If
\bar{k}<\bar{l}
, then the coefficients
c_{i}
’s,
2\bar{k}+1\leq i\leq r
, are all negative. In particular, we have that
c_{r}<0
.

When
{\rm min}\ \{\underline{k},\bar{l}\}=0
, the coefficients
c_{i}
’s in Equation (
8.11
) can all be chosen to be positive. In particular, we have that
c_{r}>0
.

Proof.
First consider
{\rm min}\ \{\underline{k},\bar{l}\}\neq 0
. We have the sets
A_{1}:=\{1,\cdots,\bar{k}\}
and
A_{2}:=\{r+1,\cdots,r+\underline{k}\}
, both containing integers which are adjoint. And we have the sets
B_{1}:=\{\bar{k}+1,\cdots,\bar{k}+\bar{l}\}
and
B_{2}:=\{r+\underline{k}+1,\cdots,r+\underline{k}+\underline{l}\}
, both containing integers which are not adjoint.

We will pair the numbers in
A_{1}\cup A_{2}
, with numbers in
B_{1}\cup B_{2}
, since the cardinality of both sets are the same.
Assume
\bar{k}\geq\bar{l}
. Define a bijective map
G:A_{1}\cup A_{2}\longrightarrow B_{1}\cup B_{2}
by

\displaystyle G(a)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}a+\bar{k},&\hbox{$1\leq a\leq\bar{l}$;}\\
a+\bar{k}+\underline{k},&\hbox{$\bar{l}+1\leq a\leq\bar{k}$;}\\
a+\underline{k}+\bar{k}-\bar{l}\equiv a+\underline{l},&\hbox{$r+1\leq a\leq r+\underline{k}$.}\end{array}\right.

Using this map, we see that we can pair in such a way that each
\delta_{i}\in A_{1}\cup A_{2}
is paired with an unique
\epsilon_{i}\in B_{1}\cup B_{2}
, with each
\delta_{i}<\epsilon_{i}
. Equation (
8.9
) holds for all the
1\leq\delta_{i}\leq\bar{k}
and
r+1\leq\delta_{i}\leq r+\underline{k}
. The map
G
shows that all the
c_{i}
’s in Equation (
8.11
), except at
i=r
, are all positive integers. But if we assume
\bar{k}>\bar{l}
, we will have
G:\bar{l}+1\mapsto r+\underline{k}+1
, thus we see that
c_{r}>0
.

Assume
\bar{k}<\bar{l}
. Define a bijective map
\tilde{G}:A_{1}\cup A_{2}\longrightarrow B_{1}\cup B_{2}
by

\displaystyle\tilde{G}(a)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}a+\bar{k},&\hbox{$1\leq a\leq\bar{k}$;}\\
a+\bar{k}-\bar{l},&\hbox{$r+1\leq a\leq r+\bar{l}-\bar{k}$;}\\
a+\bar{k}-\bar{l}+\underline{k}\equiv a+\underline{l},&\hbox{$r+\bar{l}-\bar{k}+1\leq a\leq r+\underline{k}$.}\end{array}\right.

In this case, we see that for
r+1\leq\delta_{i}\leq r+\bar{l}-\underline{k}
, we have that
\delta_{i}>\tilde{G}(\delta_{i})=\epsilon_{i}
. Equation (
8.10
) holds instead, for such
\delta_{i}
and corresponding
\epsilon_{i}
. Thus, the coefficients
c_{i}
’s,
2\bar{k}+1\leq i\leq r
in Equation (
8.11
) are negative. In particular, we have that
c_{r}<0
.

Now we consider
{\rm min}\ \{\underline{k},\bar{l}\}=0
. When
\bar{l}=0
, this implies that
r=s
and thus
r=\bar{k}=\underline{l}=s
. Define a bijective map
\hat{G}:\{1,\cdots,r\}\longrightarrow\{r+1,\cdots,2r\}
as
G(a)=a+r
. Then clearly,
a<\hat{G}(a)
and since
\hat{G}:r\mapsto r+s=2r
, all the coefficients in Equation (
8.11
) are strictly positive. In particular,
c_{r}>0
.

When
\underline{k}=0
, then we must have
\bar{k}=\bar{l}+\underline{l}
. Define a bijective map
\dot{G}
that sends
a
to
a+\bar{k}
, for
1\leq a\leq\bar{k}
. Again,
a<\dot{G}(a)
and because
\dot{G}:\bar{k}\mapsto r+s
, all the coefficients in Equation (
8.11
) are strictly positive. In particular,
c_{r}>0
.

Remark 8.18

When
\bar{k}=\bar{l}
, we see that
c_{r}=0
in the proof, which implies that the support in Lemma
8.14
is at
\{\vec{q}=\vec{0}\}
. This will pose a problem later, when we try to prove the Clustering Theorem
8.34
. Hence, we do not consider this particular case.

When
\bar{k}=0
or
\underline{l}=0
, then
\mathscr{W}^{n}\equiv 0
, which is trivial.

Remark 8.19

Assume the conditions in Proposition
8.17
hold. We will further assume one of the following cases hold:

1.

{\rm min}\ \{\underline{k},\bar{l}\}=0
; or

2.

{\rm min}\ \{\underline{k},\bar{l}\}\neq 0
and
\bar{k}>\bar{l}
; or

3.

{\rm min}\ \{\underline{k},\bar{l}\}\neq 0
,
\bar{k}<\bar{l}
and
2\bar{k}+1\leq s
.

When we assume one of the above 3 cases holds, Proposition
8.17
says that
{\rm sgn}(c_{s})={\rm sgn}(c_{r})
.
Then the supports described in Remark
8.15
are on respective positive and negative cones in energy-momentum space. The fact that the supports are separated is key to prove the Clustering Decomposition Property, as demonstrated in
[
3
]
and
[
22
]
.

In general, it is not true that

\mathscr{W}^{n}\left(\{\vec{x}_{\tau}\}_{\tau=1}^{r},\{\vec{x}_{\theta}\}_{\theta=r+1}^{r+s}\right)=\pm\mathscr{W}^{n}\left(\{\vec{x}_{\theta}\}_{\theta=r+1}^{r+s},\{\vec{x}_{\tau}\}_{\tau=1}^{r}\right).

The commutation and anti-commutation relations in Lemmas
5.15
,
5.18
and
5.20
hold only for real or purely imaginary functions respectively. Thus, we need to consider in a similar manner.

Remark 8.20

Proposition
8.29
will imply that the above equality holds, provided
x_{\theta}^{+}-x_{\tau}^{+}\neq 0
for any
1\leq\tau\leq r
and
r+1\leq\theta\leq r+s
.

Notation 8.21

For a natural number
r
, let the set
X_{r}:=\{1,2,\cdots,r\}
. Decompose
X_{r}
into 2 sets
\kappa_{r}
,
\bar{\kappa}_{r}
, i.e.
\kappa_{r}\cup\bar{\kappa}_{r}=X_{r}
and
\kappa_{r}\cap\bar{\kappa}_{r}=\emptyset
, one of it possibly empty. Let
\Omega_{r}
denote the set containing all such decompositions of
X_{r}
.

Recall we have two sets
\{f_{1},\cdots,f_{r}\}
and
\{g_{1},\cdots,g_{s}\}
. Assume that each
f_{i}
(
g_{j}
) is either real or purely imaginary. This defines 2 disjoint sets
\kappa_{r}
(
\pi_{s}
), and
\bar{\kappa}_{r}
(
\bar{\pi}_{s}
), whereby
f_{i}
(
g_{j}
) is real if
i\in\kappa_{r}
(
j\in\pi_{s}
), otherwise
f_{i}
(
g_{j}
) is purely imaginary if
i\in\bar{\kappa}_{r}
(
j\in\bar{\pi}_{s}
).

Then, we will write for
(\kappa_{r},\bar{\kappa}_{r})\in\Omega_{r}
,
(\pi_{s},\bar{\pi}_{s})\in\Omega_{s}
, tempered distributions such that

\displaystyle\mathscr{W}_{\kappa_{r},\pi_{s}}^{n}

\displaystyle:f_{1}\otimes_{{\mathbb{R}}}\cdots\otimes_{{\mathbb{R}}}f_{r}\otimes_{{\mathbb{R}}}g_{1}\otimes_{{\mathbb{R}}}\cdots\otimes_{{\mathbb{R}}}g_{s}\longmapsto\left\langle A_{r}^{n}P_{0}B_{s}^{n}1,1\right\rangle,

\displaystyle\mathscr{W}_{\pi_{s},\kappa_{r}}^{n}

\displaystyle:g_{1}\otimes_{{\mathbb{R}}}\cdots\otimes_{{\mathbb{R}}}g_{s}\otimes_{{\mathbb{R}}}f_{1}\otimes_{{\mathbb{R}}}\cdots\otimes_{{\mathbb{R}}}f_{r}\longmapsto\left\langle C_{r}^{n}P_{0}D_{s}^{n}1,1\right\rangle,

for these particular sets
\{f_{1},\cdots,f_{r}\}
and
\{g_{1},\cdots,g_{s}\}
. Refer back to Equations (
8.3
) and (
8.4
).

Remark 8.22

Recall from Notation
8.5
, we have the sets
\{f_{1},\cdots,f_{r}\}
and
\{g_{1},\cdots,g_{s}\}
. Write for
\tau=1,\cdots,r
,
f_{\tau}=\underline{f}_{\tau}+i\overline{f}_{\tau}
, whereby
\underline{f}_{\tau}={\rm Re}\ f_{\tau}
and
\overline{f}_{\tau}={\rm Im}\ f_{\tau}
. Similarly, write for
\theta=1,\cdots,s
,
g_{\theta}=\underline{g}_{\theta}+i\overline{g}_{\theta}
, whereby
\underline{g}_{\theta}={\rm Re}\ g_{\theta}
and
\overline{g}_{\theta}={\rm Im}\ g_{\theta}
.

Refer to Notation
8.21
. We can now understand

\displaystyle\int_{{{\mathbb{R}}}^{4}\times\cdots\times{{\mathbb{R}}}^{4}}\mathscr{W}^{n}\left(\{\vec{x}_{\tau}\}_{\tau=1}^{r},\{\vec{x}_{\theta}\}_{\theta=r+1}^{r+s}\right)\bigotimes_{\tau=1}^{r+s}p_{\tau}(\vec{x}_{\tau})\cdot\prod_{\tau=1}^{r+s}d\vec{x}_{\tau}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{(\kappa_r, \bar{\kappa}_r) \in\Omega_r }{(\pi_s, \bar{\pi}_s) \in\Omega_s}}\int_{{{\mathbb{R}}}^{4}\times\cdots\times{{\mathbb{R}}}^{4}}\mathscr{W}_{\kappa_{r},\pi_{s}}^{n}\left(\{\vec{x}_{\tau}\}_{\tau=1}^{r},\{\vec{x}_{\theta}\}_{\theta=r+1}^{r+s}\right)\prod_{\tau=1}^{r+s}q^{\{\kappa_{r},\pi_{s}\}}_{\tau}(\vec{x}_{\tau})\cdot\prod_{\tau=1}^{r+s}d\vec{x}_{\tau},

(8.15)

whereby for each integral term in the above sum, indexed by
\{\kappa_{r},\pi_{s}\}
,

•

for
1\leq\tau\leq r
,
q^{\{\kappa_{r},\pi_{s}\}}_{\tau}
is
\underline{f}_{\tau}
if
\tau\in\kappa_{r}
, otherwise
q^{\{\kappa_{r},\pi_{s}\}}_{\tau}
is
i\overline{f}_{\tau}\equiv\sqrt{-1}\overline{f}_{\tau}
;

•

for
r+1\leq\tau\leq r+s
,
q^{\{\kappa_{r},\pi_{s}\}}_{\tau}
is
\underline{g}_{\tau-r}
if
\tau-r\in\pi_{s}
, otherwise
q^{\{\kappa_{r},\pi_{s}\}}_{\tau}
is
i\overline{g}_{\tau-r}\equiv\sqrt{-1}\overline{g}_{\tau-r}
.

To apply the commutation or anti-commutation relations in Lemmas
5.6
,
5.15
,
5.18
and
5.20
to switch the variables, we need to assume regularity of the sets in Definition
8.16
. Making this assumption and using the above notations, we will have the following lemma. The proof involves applying successively, the commutation and anti-commutation relations in the lemmas, and the details will be left to the reader.

Lemma 8.23

Assume the two sets
\{\psi^{\alpha_{\tau},n}\}_{\tau=1}^{r}
and
\{\psi^{\beta_{\theta},n}\}_{\theta=1}^{s}
,
r\geq s
, both are regular. We have that

\mathscr{W}_{\kappa_{r},\pi_{s}}^{n}\left(\{\vec{x}_{\tau}\}_{\tau=1}^{r},\{\vec{x}_{\theta}\}_{\theta=r+1}^{r+s}\right)=\pm\mathscr{W}_{\pi_{s},\kappa_{r}}^{n}\left(\{\vec{x}_{\theta}\}_{\theta=r+1}^{r+s},\{\vec{x}_{\tau}\}_{\tau=1}^{r}\right),

(8.16)

provided for any
1\leq\tau\leq r
,
r+1\leq\theta\leq r+s
, we have
0\neq\vec{x}_{\tau}-\vec{x}_{\theta}
lies in the hyperplane spanned by
\{\hat{P}(\rho_{n})e_{0}+\hat{H}(\rho_{n})e_{1},e_{2},e_{3}\}
. The
\pm
sign depends on how many anti-commutation relations in Lemmas
5.15
,
5.18
and
5.20
are applied.

8.2
Proof of Cluster Decomposition Property

Definition 8.24

Let
\varphi_{1}
,
\varphi_{2}
be Schwartz functions on
{{\mathbb{R}}}^{4r}
and
{{\mathbb{R}}}^{4s}
respectively,
r\geq s
. Define

\displaystyle T_{1}^{n}

\displaystyle:=\int_{{{\mathbb{R}}}^{4}\times\cdots\times{{\mathbb{R}}}^{4}}\prod_{\tau=1}^{r}d\vec{x}_{\tau}\cdot\varphi_{1}\left(\{\vec{x}_{\tau}\}_{\tau=1}^{r}\right)\cdot\prod_{\tau=1}^{r}\psi^{\alpha_{\tau},n}(\vec{x}_{\tau}),

\displaystyle T_{2}^{n}

\displaystyle:=\int_{{{\mathbb{R}}}^{4}\times\cdots\times{{\mathbb{R}}}^{4}}\prod_{\theta=r+1}^{r+s}d\vec{x}_{\theta}\cdot\varphi_{2}\left(\{\vec{x}_{\theta}\}_{\theta=r+1}^{r+s}\right)\cdot\prod_{\theta=r+1}^{r+s}\psi^{\beta_{\theta-r},n}(\vec{x}_{\theta}).

And define

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{1}^{n}

\displaystyle\left(\{\vec{x}_{\tau}\}_{\tau=s+1}^{r}\right)

\displaystyle:=\int_{{{\mathbb{R}}}^{4}\times\cdots\times{{\mathbb{R}}}^{4}}\prod_{\theta=r+1}^{r+s}d\vec{x}_{\theta}\cdot\varphi_{1}\left(\{\vec{x}_{\tau}\}_{\tau=s+1}^{r},\{\vec{x}_{\theta}\}_{\theta=r+1}^{r+s}\right)\cdot\prod_{\theta=r+1}^{r+s}\psi^{\beta_{\theta-r},n}(\vec{x}_{\theta}),

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{2}^{n}

\displaystyle\left(\{\vec{x}_{\tau}\}_{\tau=s+1}^{r}\right)

\displaystyle:=\int_{{{\mathbb{R}}}^{4}\times\cdots\times{{\mathbb{R}}}^{4}}\prod_{\tau=1}^{s}d\vec{x}_{\tau}\cdot\varphi_{2}\left(\{\vec{x}_{\tau}\}_{\tau=1}^{s}\right)\cdot\prod_{\tau=1}^{r}\psi^{\alpha_{\tau},n}(\vec{x}_{\tau}),

whereby
\psi^{\alpha_{\tau},n}(\vec{x}_{\tau})
is either
\phi^{\alpha_{\tau},n}(\vec{x}_{\tau})

or its adjoint operator. Likewise for
\psi^{\beta_{\theta-r},n}(\vec{x}_{\theta})
.

We will need to assume the following:

•

both the sets
\{\psi^{\alpha_{\tau},n}\}_{\tau=1}^{r}
and
\{\psi^{\beta_{\theta},n}\}_{\theta=1}^{s}
are regular, as defined in Definition
8.16
;

•

\sum_{\tau=1}^{r}\chi(\tau)+\sum_{\theta=r+1}^{r+s}\chi(\theta)=0
, i.e. there are an equal number of creation and annihilation operators.

Suppose
\vec{a}\in{{\mathbb{R}}}^{4}
. Define

\displaystyle T_{1}^{n}\left(\vec{a}\right)

\displaystyle:=U(\vec{a},1)T_{1}^{n}U(\vec{a},1)^{-1},

\displaystyle T_{2}^{n}\left(\vec{a}\right)

\displaystyle:=U(\vec{a},1)T_{2}^{n}U(\vec{a},1)^{-1}.

By default, we will write
T_{i}^{n}\equiv T_{i}^{n}(\vec{0})
,
i=1,2
.
Define similarly

\mathcal{T}_{1}^{n}\left(\{\vec{x}_{\tau}\}_{\tau=s+1}^{r},\vec{a}\right),\quad\mathcal{T}_{2}^{n}\left(\{\vec{x}_{\tau}\}_{\tau=s+1}^{r},\vec{a}\right),

replacing
T_{i}^{n}
with
\mathcal{T}_{i}^{n}
respectively in the above formulas.

Refer to Definition
8.24
. Define

\displaystyle T_{1}:=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}c_{1,n}T_{1}^{n},\quad T_{2}:=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}c_{2,n}T_{2}^{n},

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{1}\left(\{\vec{x}_{\tau}\}_{\tau=s+1}^{r}\right):=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}c_{1,n}\mathcal{T}_{1}^{n}\left(\{\vec{x}_{\tau}\}_{\tau=s+1}^{r}\right),

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{2}\left(\{\vec{x}_{\tau}\}_{\tau=s+1}^{r}\right):=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}c_{2,n}\mathcal{T}_{2}^{n}\left(\{\vec{x}_{\tau}\}_{\tau=s+1}^{r}\right),

each
\{c_{i,n}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
is a sequence of numbers in
l^{2}
space, such that
\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}|c_{i,n}|^{2}C(\rho_{n})^{2k}<\infty
, for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
.

Remark 8.25

Note that for
j=1,2
,
|T_{j}^{n}1|^{2}=O(C(\rho_{n}))
,
|\mathcal{T}_{j}^{n}1|^{2}=O(C(\rho_{n}))
. Because for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}|c_{j,n}|^{2}C(\rho_{n})^{2k}<\infty
, we see that

|\hat{H}^{k}T_{j}(\cdot,\vec{a})1|^{2}\leq\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}C(\rho_{n})^{2k}|c_{j,n}|^{2}|T_{j}^{n}(\cdot)1|^{2}<\infty.

(8.17)

Similar bound for
\mathcal{T}_{j}
.
This is required to prove the Clustering Decomposition Property
8.32
and Clustering Theorem
8.34
.

Indeed,

\displaystyle\Big\langle

\displaystyle T_{1}\left(\vec{y}\right)T_{2}\left(\vec{x}\right)1,1\Big\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}c_{1,n}c_{2,n}\left\langle U(\vec{y},1)T_{1}^{n}U(\vec{y},1)^{-1}U(\vec{x},1)T_{2}^{n}U(\vec{x},1)^{-1}1,1\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}c_{1,n}c_{2,n}\left\langle T_{1}^{n}U(\vec{x}-\vec{y},1)T_{2}^{n}1,1\right\rangle,

which converges by Equation (
8.17
).

Similarly,

\displaystyle\Big\langle

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{2}\left(\{\vec{x}_{\tau}\}_{\tau=s+1}^{r},\vec{x}\right)\mathcal{T}_{1}\left(\{\vec{x}_{\tau}\}_{\tau=s+1}^{r},\vec{y}\right)1,1\Big\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}c_{1,n}c_{2,n}\left\langle\mathcal{T}_{2}^{n}\left(\{\vec{x}_{\tau}\}_{\tau=s+1}^{r},\vec{x}\right)\mathcal{T}_{1}^{n}\left(\{\vec{x}_{\tau}\}_{\tau=s+1}^{r},\vec{y}\right)1,1\right\rangle,

which also converges.

Definition 8.26

For
\vec{a}=\vec{x}-\vec{y}\in S_{0}
,
we will define

\displaystyle h_{12}^{n}\left(\vec{a}\right):=

\displaystyle\left\langle T_{1}^{n}\left(\vec{y}\right)P_{0}T_{2}^{n}\left(\vec{x}\right)1,1\right\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\langle T_{1}^{n}U(\vec{y},1)^{-1}U(\vec{x},1)T_{2}^{n}1,1\right\rangle-\langle T_{1}^{n}1,1\rangle\langle T_{2}^{n}1,1\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\langle T_{1}^{n}U(\vec{a},1)T_{2}^{n}1,1\right\rangle-\langle T_{1}^{n}1,1\rangle\langle T_{2}^{n}1,1\rangle.

Similarly, define (
s^{+}=s+1
)

\displaystyle h_{21}^{n}

\displaystyle\left(\vec{a}\right)

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\int_{{{\mathbb{R}}}^{4}\times\cdots\times{{\mathbb{R}}}^{4}}\prod_{\tau=s^{+}}^{r}d\vec{x}_{\tau}\ \left\langle\mathcal{T}_{2}^{n}\left(\{\vec{x}_{\tau}\}_{\tau=s^{+}}^{r},\vec{x}\right)P_{0}\mathcal{T}_{1}^{n}\left(\{\vec{x}_{\tau}\}_{\tau=s^{+}}^{r},\vec{y}\right)1,1\right\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\langle\int_{{{\mathbb{R}}}^{4(r-s)}}\prod_{\tau=s^{+}}^{r}d\vec{x}_{\tau}\ \mathcal{T}_{2}^{n}\left(\{\vec{x}_{\tau}\}_{\tau=s^{+}}^{r}\right)U(-\vec{a},1)\mathcal{T}_{1}^{n}\left(\{\vec{x}_{\tau}\}_{\tau=s^{+}}^{r}\right)1,1\right\rangle

\displaystyle-\int_{{{\mathbb{R}}}^{4(r-s)}}\prod_{\tau=s^{+}}^{r}d\vec{x}_{\tau}\left\langle\mathcal{T}_{1}^{n}\left(\{\vec{x}_{\tau}\}_{\tau=s^{+}}^{r}\right)1,1\right\rangle\left\langle\mathcal{T}_{2}^{n}\left(\{\vec{x}_{\tau}\}_{\tau=s^{+}}^{r}\right)1,1\right\rangle.

Using Definition
8.11
, we will extend the definitions of
h_{12}^{n}\left(\vec{a}\right)
and
h_{21}^{n}\left(\vec{a}\right)
, for
\vec{a}\in S_{0}
, to all of
\vec{a}\in{{\mathbb{R}}}^{4}
.

From Equation (
8.7
), we will have (
r^{+}=r+1
)

\displaystyle h_{12}^{n}

\displaystyle\left(\vec{a}\right)

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\int_{{{\mathbb{R}}}^{4}\times\cdots\times{{\mathbb{R}}}^{4}}\prod_{\tau=1}^{r+s}d\vec{x}_{\tau}\ \mathscr{W}^{n}\left(\{\vec{x}_{\tau}\}_{\tau=1}^{r},\{\vec{x}_{\theta}+\vec{a}\}_{\theta=r^{+}}^{r+s}\right)\varphi_{1}\left(\{\vec{x}_{\tau}\}_{\tau=1}^{r}\right)\varphi_{2}\left(\{\vec{x}_{\theta}\}_{\theta=r^{+}}^{r+s}\right).

(8.18)

Similarly, we will define (
s^{+}=s+1
)

\displaystyle h_{21}^{n}

\displaystyle\left(\vec{a}\right)

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\int_{{{\mathbb{R}}}^{4}\times\cdots\times{{\mathbb{R}}}^{4}}\prod_{\tau=1}^{r+s}d\vec{x}_{\tau}\ \mathscr{W}^{n}\left(\{\vec{x}_{\theta}+\vec{a}\}_{\theta=r^{+}}^{r+s},\{\vec{x}_{\tau}\}_{\tau=1}^{r}\right)\varphi_{1}\left(\{\vec{x}_{\tau}\}_{\tau=1}^{r}\right)\varphi_{2}\left(\{\vec{x}_{\theta}\}_{\theta=r^{+}}^{r+s}\right).

(8.19)

When
\vec{a}\in S_{0}
, the LHS of Equations (
8.18
) and (
8.19
) are equal to

\displaystyle\left\langle T_{1}^{n}U(\vec{a},1)T_{2}^{n}1,1\right\rangle-\langle T_{1}^{n}1,1\rangle\langle T_{2}^{n}1,1\rangle\quad{\rm and}

\displaystyle\left\langle\int_{{{\mathbb{R}}}^{4(r-s)}}\prod_{\tau=s^{+}}^{r}d\vec{x}_{\tau}\ \mathcal{T}_{2}^{n}\left(\{\vec{x}_{\tau}\}_{\tau=s^{+}}^{r}\right)U(-\vec{a},1)\mathcal{T}_{1}^{n}\left(\{\vec{x}_{\tau}\}_{\tau=s^{+}}^{r}\right)1,1\right\rangle

\displaystyle-\int_{{{\mathbb{R}}}^{4(r-s)}}\prod_{\tau=s^{+}}^{r}d\vec{x}_{\tau}\left\langle\mathcal{T}_{1}^{n}\left(\{\vec{x}_{\tau}\}_{\tau=s^{+}}^{r}\right)1,1\right\rangle\left\langle\mathcal{T}_{2}^{n}\left(\{\vec{x}_{\tau}\}_{\tau=s^{+}}^{r}\right)1,1\right\rangle

respectively.

Definition 8.27

Define for any
\vec{a}\in{{\mathbb{R}}}^{4}
,

h_{12}\left(\vec{a}\right):=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}c_{1,n}c_{2,n}h_{12}^{n}\left(\vec{a}\right),\quad h_{21}\left(\vec{a}\right):=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}c_{1,n}c_{2,n}h_{21}^{n}\left(\vec{a}\right).

Definition 8.28

Recall from Definition
7.13
, we defined the set
\{\hat{H}(\rho_{n}),\hat{P}(\rho_{n})\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
, such that
\hat{H}(\rho_{n})^{2}-\hat{P}(\rho_{n})^{2}=m_{n}^{2}
, with
m_{n}>0
as the mass gap in
\mathscr{H}(\rho_{n})
.

Let
\Lambda_{n}
be a Lorentz transformation that maps the standard orthonormal basis
e_{a}\mapsto\tilde{f}_{a}^{n}:=\Lambda_{n}e_{a}
, whereby
\tilde{f}_{0}^{n}
and
\tilde{f}_{1}^{n}
are

\frac{\hat{H}(\rho_{n})}{m_{n}}e_{0}+\frac{\hat{P}(\rho_{n})}{m_{n}}e_{1},\quad\frac{\hat{P}(\rho_{n})}{m_{n}}e_{0}+\frac{\hat{H}(\rho_{n})}{m_{n}}e_{1}

respectively, and
\tilde{f}_{2}^{n}=e_{2}
,
\tilde{f}_{3}^{n}=e_{3}
, which together spans the plane
S_{0}
.

Proposition 8.29

Write
\vec{a}=(a^{-},a^{+})
, whereby
a^{-}=(a^{0},a^{1})
,
a^{+}=(a^{2},a^{3})
. If
x_{\tau}^{+}-x_{\theta}^{+}\neq a^{+}
for any
1\leq\tau\leq r
and
r+1\leq\theta\leq r+s
, then we have that

\mathscr{W}^{n}\left(\{\vec{x}_{\tau}\}_{\tau=1}^{r},\{\vec{x}_{\theta}+\vec{a}\}_{\theta=r+1}^{r+s}\right)=0.

Proof.
It is not difficult to see from Equation (
8.13
) that

\displaystyle\mathscr{W}^{n}

\displaystyle\left(\{\vec{x}_{\tau}\}_{\tau=1}^{r},\{\vec{x}_{\theta}+\vec{a}\}_{\theta=r+1}^{r+s}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e^{-i\vec{a}\cdot c_{r}m_{n}\tilde{f}_{0}^{n}}\prod_{\theta=1}^{r+s}E(x_{\theta}^{-})\cdot\mathscr{W}_{0}^{n}\left(\{x^{+}_{\tau}\}_{\tau=1}^{r},\{x_{\theta}^{+}+a^{+}\}_{\theta=r+1}^{r+s}\right),

(8.20)

whereby
E(x_{\theta}^{-})=E(x_{\theta}^{0},x_{\theta}^{1})=\dfrac{e^{i\chi(\theta)[x_{\theta}^{0}\hat{H}-x_{\theta}^{1}\hat{P}]}}{2\pi}
and (
0^{-}\equiv(0,0)
)

\mathscr{W}_{0}^{n}\left(\{x^{+}_{\tau}\}_{\tau=1}^{r},\{x_{\theta}^{+}+a^{+}\}_{\theta=r+1}^{r+s}\right)=\mathscr{W}^{n}\left(\{0^{-},x^{+}_{\tau}\}_{\tau=1}^{r},\{0^{-},x_{\theta}^{+}+a^{+}\}_{\theta=r+1}^{r+s}\right).

(8.21)

Because we subtract off
\left\langle A_{r}^{n}1,1\right\rangle\left\langle B_{s}^{n}1,1\right\rangle
in Equation (
8.3
), we see that
c_{Q}
defined in Equation (
8.2
) is non-zero, only if there exists a set
A_{l}\in Q
which contains both
r
and
r+1
. From Equation (
8.7
), we have that
\mathscr{W}_{0}^{n}\equiv 0
, if
x_{\tau}^{+}-x_{\theta}^{+}\neq a^{+}
for any
1\leq\tau\leq r
and
r+1\leq\theta\leq r+s
, and this completes the proof.

Remark 8.30

From Item
2
in Lemma
8.13
, we see that
\mathscr{W}^{n}=0
iff for any partition
Q=\{A_{1},\ldots,A_{n(Q)}\}\in\Gamma
with
c_{Q}
defined in Equation (
8.2
) is non-zero, there exists some
\theta\neq r
such that
\{\theta,\theta+1\}\subset A_{l}
for some
1\leq l\leq n(Q)
and
\xi_{\theta}^{+}\neq(0,0)
, or
\xi_{r}^{+}\neq a^{+}
.

Proposition 8.31

Refer to Definition
8.28
. Recall the sets
\kappa_{r}\subseteq\{1,\cdots,r\}
,
\pi_{s}\subseteq\{1,\cdots,s\}
, as explained in Notation
8.21
. Fix
a^{2},a^{3}\in{{\mathbb{R}}}
.

Suppose one of the 3 cases hold in in Remark
8.19
and

\mathscr{W}_{\kappa_{r},\pi_{s}}^{n}\left(\{\vec{x}_{\tau}\}_{\tau=1}^{r},\{\vec{x}_{\theta}+\vec{a}\}_{\theta=r+1}^{r+s}\right)=\pm\mathscr{W}_{\pi_{s},\kappa_{r}}^{n}\left(\{\vec{x}_{\theta}+\vec{a}\}_{\theta=r+1}^{r+s},\{\vec{x}_{\tau}\}_{\tau=1}^{r}\right)

holds for any
a^{0}\in{{\mathbb{R}}}
, such that
\vec{a}=a^{0}\tilde{f}_{0}^{n}+a^{2}\tilde{f}_{2}^{n}+a^{3}\tilde{f}_{3}^{n}
is a space-like vector. Then it is necessary and sufficient that
\mathscr{W}_{\kappa_{r},\pi_{s}}^{n}\left(\{\vec{x}_{\tau}\}_{\tau=1}^{r},\{\vec{x}_{\theta}+\vec{a}\}_{\theta=r+1}^{r+s}\right)
and
\mathscr{W}_{\pi_{s},\kappa_{r}}^{n}\left(\{\vec{x}_{\theta}+\vec{a}\}_{\theta=r+1}^{r+s},\{\vec{x}_{\tau}\}_{\tau=1}^{r}\right)
both must vanish.

Proof.
We only consider when the sign is a plus. The other case is similar. From Equation (
8.13
), we have

\displaystyle\mathscr{W}_{\kappa_{r},\pi_{s}}^{n}\left(\{\vec{x}_{\tau}\}_{\tau=1}^{r},\{\vec{x}_{\theta}+\vec{a}\}_{\theta=r+1}^{r+s}\right)=

\displaystyle e^{ia^{0}c_{r}m_{n}}\mathscr{W}_{\kappa_{r},\pi_{s}}^{n}\left(\{\vec{x}_{\tau}\}_{\tau=1}^{r},\{\vec{x}_{\theta}+a^{+}\}_{\theta=r+1}^{r+s}\right),

and

\displaystyle\mathscr{W}_{\pi_{s},\kappa_{r}}^{n}\left(\{\vec{x}_{\theta}+\vec{a}\}_{\theta=r+1}^{r+s},\{\vec{x}_{\tau}\}_{\tau=1}^{r}\right)=

\displaystyle e^{-ia^{0}c_{s}m_{n}}\mathscr{W}_{\pi_{s},\kappa_{r}}^{n}\left(\{\vec{x}_{\theta}+a^{+}\}_{\theta=r+1}^{r+s},\{\vec{x}_{\tau}\}_{\tau=1}^{r}\right).

Because we assume one of the 3 cases hold,
c_{s}
and
c_{r}
have the same sign and thus the preceding two equations are not equal for general values of
a^{0}
, unless both terms are equal to zero.

Recall from Definition
3.3
, we defined the norm
\parallel\cdot\parallel_{p,q}
for Schwartz functions on
{{\mathbb{R}}}^{4}
. We can generalize this definition, for a Schwartz function defined on the Euclidean space
{{\mathbb{R}}}^{r}
, for any
r\in\mathbb{N}
.

By considering
h_{12}-h_{21}
, we can mimic the proof of Theorem 3-4 (Cluster Decomposition Property) in
[
3
]
, or the lemma in
[
19
]
, to prove that
\left|h_{12}\left(\vec{a}\right)\right|
decays at the rate of
\frac{1}{|a^{+}|^{p}}
for
|a^{+}|
large and for any
p\in\mathbb{N}
. However, using Lemma
8.23
is inadequate to prove this result. We instead need local commutativity as given by Proposition
8.31
and assuming one of the 3 cases hold in Remark
8.19
, to prove the result. We will however, prove the following slightly stronger result, using Proposition
8.29
and without assuming any of the cases in Remark
8.19
hold.

Theorem 8.32

(Cluster Decomposition Property)

Recall
m_{0}
is the mass gap of the 4-dimensional quantum Yang-Mills theory. Suppose the assumptions in Definition
8.24
hold, i.e. regularity of the two sets and equal number of creation and annihilation operators. Let
\vec{a}=\sum_{b=0}^{3}a^{b}e_{b}
be a space-like vector, with
a^{-}\equiv(a^{0},a^{1})
and
a^{+}\equiv(a^{2},a^{3})\neq(0,0)
. Then, we have for some positive constant
C
,

\displaystyle\left|h_{12}\left(\vec{a}\right)\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}c_{1,n}c_{2,n}h_{12}^{n}\left(\vec{a}\right)\ \right|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{m_{0}^{l}+|a^{+}|^{k}}\left(\parallel\varphi_{1}\parallel_{p,q}\parallel\varphi_{2}\parallel_{p,q}\right),

(8.22)

for any
k,l\in\mathbb{N}
, provided
p,q\in\mathbb{N}
are large enough.

Proof.
Recall the sets
\kappa_{r}\subseteq\{1,\cdots,r\}
,
\pi_{s}\subseteq\{1,\cdots,s\}
, as explained in Notation
8.21
. Consider the real vector spaces
\mathcal{Y}_{\kappa_{r}}
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{\pi_{s}}
, each spanned by tensor products of functions of the form

f_{1}\otimes_{{\mathbb{R}}}f_{2}\otimes_{{\mathbb{R}}}\cdots\otimes_{{\mathbb{R}}}f_{r}\quad{\rm and}\quad g_{1}\otimes_{{\mathbb{R}}}\cdots\otimes_{{\mathbb{R}}}g_{s}

respectively, whereby each
f_{i}
(
g_{j}
) is real if
i\in\kappa_{r}
(
j\in\pi_{s}
), purely imaginary if
i\notin\kappa_{r}
(
j\notin\pi_{s}
).

Instead of proving the result for general
\varphi_{i}
’s, from Equation (
8.15
), it suffices to prove it for
\varphi_{1}\in\mathcal{Y}_{\kappa_{r}}
and
\varphi_{2}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{\pi_{s}}
, whereby we need to change the definition of
h_{12}^{n}
, by replacing
\mathscr{W}^{n}
with
\mathscr{W}_{\kappa_{r},\pi_{s}}^{n}
in Equation (
8.18
).

Without any loss of generality, we assume that
C(\rho_{n})>1
, otherwise we can replace
C(\rho_{n})
in the following inequalities with
(1+C(\rho_{n}))
instead. Write

\varphi\left(\{\vec{x}_{\theta}\}_{\theta=1}^{r+s}\right)=\varphi_{1}\left(\{\vec{x}_{\tau}\}_{\tau=1}^{r}\right)\otimes_{{\mathbb{R}}}\varphi_{2}\left(\{\vec{x}_{\theta}\}_{\theta=r^{+}}^{r+s}\right).

Let
q^{-}=(q^{0},q^{1})
be coordinates pertaining to the
\{e_{0},e_{1}\}
basis, with
x_{\tau}^{-}\cdot q_{\tau}^{-}=-x_{\tau}^{0}q_{\tau}^{0}+x_{\tau}^{1}q_{\tau}^{1}
, and define the following partial Fourier Transforms,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\tilde{\varphi}_{1}\left(\{q_{\tau}^{-},x_{\tau}^{+}\}_{\tau=1}^{r}\right)&:=\int_{{{\mathbb{R}}}^{2r}}\prod_{\tau=1}^{r}\frac{e^{-i\chi(\tau)x_{\tau}^{-}\cdot q_{\tau}^{-}}}{2\pi}\cdot\varphi_{1}\left(\{x_{\tau}^{-},x_{\tau}^{+}\}_{\tau=1}^{r}\right)\ \prod_{\tau=1}^{r+s}dx_{\tau}^{-},\\
\tilde{\varphi}_{2}\left(\{q_{\theta}^{-},x_{\theta}^{+}\}_{\theta=r+1}^{r+s}\right)&:=\int_{{{\mathbb{R}}}^{2s}}\prod_{\theta=r+1}^{r+s}\frac{e^{-i\chi(\theta)x_{\theta}^{-}\cdot q_{\theta}^{-}}}{2\pi}\cdot\varphi_{2}\left(\{x_{\theta}^{-},x_{\theta}^{+}\}_{\theta=r+1}^{r+s}\right)\ \prod_{\theta=r+1}^{r+s}dx_{\theta}^{-},\end{split}

(8.23)

and

\tilde{\varphi}\left(\{q_{\tau}^{-},x_{\tau}^{+}\}_{\tau=1}^{r+s}\right):=\tilde{\varphi}_{1}\left(\{q_{\tau}^{-},x_{\tau}^{+}\}_{\tau=1}^{r}\right)\tilde{\varphi}_{2}\left(\{q_{\theta}^{-},x_{\theta}^{+}\}_{\theta=r+1}^{r+s}\right).

Suppose we let
H_{n}^{-}=(\hat{H}(\rho_{n}),\hat{P}(\rho_{n}))
and recall we defined
\mathscr{W}_{0}^{n}
in Equation (
8.21
). Then, we see that

\displaystyle h_{12}^{n}(\vec{a})

\displaystyle=e^{-ic_{r}m_{n}\vec{a}\cdot\tilde{f}_{0}^{n}}\int_{{{\mathbb{R}}}^{2(r+s)}}\prod_{\tau=1}^{r+s}dx_{\tau}^{+}\ \mathscr{W}_{0}^{n}\left(\{x_{\tau}^{+}\}_{\tau=1}^{r},\{x_{\theta}^{+}+a^{+}\}_{\theta=r^{+}}^{r+s}\right)\tilde{\varphi}\left(\{H_{n}^{-},x_{\tau}^{+}\}_{\tau=1}^{r+s}\right),

which shows that it is a constant in the
\tilde{f}_{1}^{n}
direction.

Since the Fourier transform of a Schwartz function remains a Schwartz function, we see that for some
\bar{n}\in\mathbb{N}
, independent of
n
,

\left|\tilde{\varphi}\left(\{q_{\theta}^{-},x_{\theta}^{+}\}_{\theta=1}^{r+s}\right)\right|\leq C(\rho_{n})^{\bar{n}}\frac{\parallel\varphi\parallel_{\hat{k},\hat{l}}}{\sum_{\theta=1}^{r+s}(|q_{\theta}^{0}|^{2}+|q_{\theta}^{1}|^{2})^{l/2}+\left(|x_{\theta}^{2}|^{2}+|x_{\theta}^{3}|^{2}\right)^{k/2}},

for some
\hat{k}\geq k
,
\hat{l}\geq l
large enough. In particular,
(\hat{H}(\rho_{n})^{2}+\hat{P}(\rho_{n})^{2})^{l/2}\geq m_{n}^{l}
. Thus, we have that

\left|\tilde{\varphi}\left(\{H_{n}^{-},x_{\theta}^{+}\}_{\theta=1}^{r+s}\right)\right|\leq C(\rho_{n})^{\bar{n}}\frac{\parallel\varphi\parallel_{\hat{k},\hat{l}}}{\sum_{\theta=1}^{r+s}m_{n}^{l}+\left(x_{\theta}^{2,2}+x_{\theta}^{3,2}\right)^{k/2}}.

There exists a set containing polynomially bounded continuous functions
\{G_{\vec{m}}^{n}:\ 0\leq|\vec{m}|\leq\mathcal{N}\}
, such that

\displaystyle e^{-ic_{r}m_{n}\vec{a}\cdot\tilde{f}_{0}^{n}}\sum_{|\vec{m}|\leq\mathcal{N}}\int_{{{\mathbb{R}}}^{2(r+s)}}

\displaystyle\prod_{\tau=1}^{r+s}dx_{\tau}^{+}D^{\vec{m}}G_{\vec{m}}^{n}\left(\{x_{\tau}^{+}\}_{\tau=1}^{r+s};a^{+}\right)\tilde{\varphi}\left(\{H_{n}^{-},x_{\theta}^{+}\}_{\theta=1}^{r+s}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle h_{12}^{n}\left(\vec{a}\right),

and

\displaystyle\sum_{|\vec{m}|\leq\mathcal{N}}D^{\vec{m}}G_{\vec{m}}^{n}\left(\{\{x_{\tau}^{+}\}_{\tau=1}^{r+s};a^{+}\right)\equiv

\displaystyle\mathscr{W}_{0}^{n}\left(\{x_{\tau}^{+}\}_{\tau=1}^{r},\{x_{\theta}^{+}+a^{+}\}_{\theta=r+1}^{r+s}\right),

with

\sum_{|\vec{m}|\leq\mathcal{N}}|G_{\vec{m}}^{n}|\left(\{x_{\tau}^{+}\}_{\tau=1}^{r+s};a^{+}\right)\leq C(\rho_{n})^{\tilde{k}}\left[|a^{+}|^{\alpha}+\left(\sum_{\tau=1}^{r+s}|x_{\tau}^{+}|^{2}\right)^{\gamma/2}\right],

for constants
\tilde{k},\alpha,\gamma
, all independent of
n
.

Choose
R_{0}=|a^{+}|/2>0
. For
0<\epsilon<|a^{+}|/2
small, the set

\{R>R_{0}-\epsilon\}:=\left\{\{x_{\tau}^{+}\}_{\tau=1}^{r+s}:\ \sum_{\tau=1}^{r+s}|x_{\tau}^{+}|^{2}>(R_{0}-\epsilon)^{2}\right\}\subset{{\mathbb{R}}}^{2(r+s)}.

Hence, we can apply Proposition
8.29
and
D^{\vec{m}}G_{\vec{m}}^{n}\left(\cdot;a^{+}\right)
vanishes on its complement. Thus we have

\displaystyle\sum_{|\vec{m}|\leq\mathcal{N}}

\displaystyle\int_{{{\mathbb{R}}}^{2(r+s)}}\prod_{\tau=1}^{r+s}dx_{\tau}^{+}\ D^{\vec{m}}G_{\vec{m}}^{n}\left(\{x_{\tau}^{+}\}_{\tau=1}^{r+s};a^{+}\right)\tilde{\varphi}\left(\{q_{\theta}^{-},x_{\theta}^{+}\}_{\theta=1}^{r+s}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{|\vec{m}|\leq\mathcal{N}}\int_{\{R>R_{0}-\epsilon\}}\prod_{\tau=1}^{r+s}dx_{\tau}^{+}\ D^{\vec{m}}G_{\vec{m}}^{n}\left(\{x_{\tau}^{+}\}_{\tau=1}^{r+s};a^{+}\right)\tilde{\varphi}\left(\{q_{\theta}^{-},x_{\theta}^{+}\}_{\theta=1}^{r+s}\right).

Using spherical coordinates,

\displaystyle\bigg|h_{12}^{n}\left(\vec{a}\right)\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{|\vec{m}|\leq\mathcal{N}}\int_{\{R>R_{0}-\epsilon\}}\prod_{\tau=1}^{r+s}dx_{\tau}^{+}\ \left|G_{\vec{m}}^{n}\left(\{x_{\tau}^{+}\}_{\tau=1}^{r+s};a^{+}\right)D^{\vec{m}}\tilde{\varphi}\left(\{H_{n}^{-},x_{\theta}^{+}\}_{\theta=1}^{r+s}\right)\right|

\displaystyle\leq C(\rho_{n})^{\tilde{K}}\int_{\{R>R_{0}-\epsilon\}}\left[|a^{+}|^{\alpha}+R^{\gamma}\right]\frac{\parallel\varphi\parallel_{p,q}}{(r+s)|m_{n}|^{l}+R^{k+\bar{l}}}\frac{R^{2(r+s)}}{R}dRd\Omega,

for some norm
\parallel\cdot\parallel_{p,q}
, and
k,l,\bar{l}
can be any natural numbers less than
p
,
q
. And
\tilde{K}\geq\tilde{k}+\bar{n}
is some natural number, independent of
n
.

By choosing
p\geq k+\bar{l},q\geq l
and
\tilde{K}
large enough, we see that the above inequality is less than some positive constant times

\displaystyle C(\rho_{n})^{\tilde{K}}\frac{\parallel\varphi_{1}\parallel_{p,q}\parallel\varphi_{2}\parallel_{p,q}}{m_{n}^{l}+|a^{+}|^{k}},

for any
|a^{+}|
.

Since the above equation holds for any non-compact
\varphi_{i}
, we have for each
n
,

\displaystyle\mathscr{W}_{\kappa_{r},\pi_{s}}^{n}

\displaystyle\left(\{\vec{x}_{\tau}\}_{\tau=1}^{r},\{\vec{x}_{\theta}+\vec{a}\}_{\theta=r^{+}}^{r+s}\right)\longrightarrow 0,

as
|a^{+}|\equiv|(a^{2},a^{3})|\rightarrow\infty
, decaying at the rate of
\dfrac{1}{m_{n}^{l}+|a^{+}|^{k}}
, for any power
k,l\in\mathbb{N}
.

For some fixed integer
\tilde{K}>0
,
C=O\left(\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}|c_{1,n}c_{2,n}|C(\rho_{n})^{\tilde{K}}\right)
from Equation (
8.17
). Since
m_{0}\leq m_{n}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we have

\displaystyle\bigg|h_{12}\left(\vec{a}\right)\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}|c_{1,n}c_{2,n}|\bigg|h_{12}^{n}\left(\vec{a}\right)\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{m_{0}^{l}+|a^{+}|^{k}}\left(\parallel\varphi_{1}\parallel_{p,q}\parallel\varphi_{2}\parallel_{p,q}\right).

Notation 8.33

Refer to Notation
8.6
. Let
\Omega_{r}
denote the set consisting of partitions in
\Gamma
, such that
Q=\{A_{1},\ldots,A_{n(Q)}\}\in\Omega_{r}
, iff there exists a (unique)
1\leq z\leq n(Q)
, such that both
r,r+1\in A_{z}
.

Recall
H_{n}^{-}=(\hat{H}(\rho_{n}),\hat{P}(\rho_{n}))
. Write for
1\leq i\leq n(Q)
,
n_{0}:=0
,
n_{i}=\sum_{k=1}^{i}|A_{k}|
and

\{y_{j}^{+}\}^{|A_{i}|}=\Big(\underbrace{H_{n}^{-},y_{i}^{+},H_{n}^{-},y_{i}^{+},\cdots,H_{n}^{-},y_{i}^{+}}_{|A_{i}|\ {\rm copies}\ {\rm of}\ (H_{n}^{-},y_{i}^{+})}\Big).

Refer to Equation (
8.23
) for the notations

\tilde{\varphi}_{1}\left(\{H_{n}^{-},x_{\tau}^{+}\}_{\tau=1}^{r}\right)\quad{\rm and}\quad\tilde{\varphi}_{2}\left(\{H_{n}^{-},x_{\theta}^{+}\}_{\theta=r+1}^{r+s}\right).

For a given partition
Q=\{A_{1},\ldots,A_{n(Q)}\}\in\Omega_{r}
, we will write

\displaystyle\tilde{\varphi}_{Q,1}^{n}\left(\{x_{\theta}^{+}\}_{\theta=n_{z-1}+1}^{r}\right)

\displaystyle:=\int_{{{\mathbb{R}}}^{2(z-1)}}\prod_{i=1}^{z-1}dy_{i}^{+}\tilde{\varphi}_{1}\left(\{y_{j}^{+}\}^{|A_{1}|},\{y_{j}^{+}\}^{|A_{2}|},\cdots,\{y_{j}^{+}\}^{|A_{z-1}|},\{H_{n}^{-},x_{\theta}^{+}\}_{\theta=n_{z-1}+1}^{r}\right).

Similarly, we will write (
z^{+}=z+1
)

\displaystyle\tilde{\varphi}_{Q,2}^{n}\left(\{x_{\theta}^{+}\}_{\theta=r^{+}}^{n_{z}}\right)

\displaystyle:=\int_{{{\mathbb{R}}}^{2(n(Q)-z)}}\prod_{i=z^{+}}^{n(Q)}dy_{i}^{+}\tilde{\varphi}_{2}\left(\{H_{n}^{-},x_{\theta}^{+}\}_{\theta=r^{+}}^{n_{z}},\{y_{j}^{+}\}^{|A_{z^{+}}|},\{y_{j}^{+}\}^{|A_{z+2}|},\cdots,\{y_{j}^{+}\}^{|A_{n(Q)}|}\right).

Note that
\{n_{z-1}+1,\cdots,r,r+1,\cdots,n_{z}\}=A_{z}\in Q
. Finally, we will write

\tilde{\varphi}_{Q,1}^{n}(x_{r}^{+}):=\tilde{\varphi}_{Q,1}^{n}\left(\{x_{r}^{+}\}_{\theta=n_{z-1}+1}^{r}\right),\quad\tilde{\varphi}_{Q,2}^{n}(x_{r+1}^{+}):=\tilde{\varphi}_{Q,2}^{n}\left(\{x_{r+1}^{+}\}_{\theta=r+1}^{n_{z}}\right).

From Equations (
8.7
) and (
8.18
), we will leave to the reader to verify that for any
\vec{a}\equiv(a^{-},a^{+})\in{{\mathbb{R}}}^{4}
,

\displaystyle h_{12}(\vec{a})=e^{-ic_{r}m_{n}\vec{a}\cdot\tilde{f}_{0}^{n}}\sum_{Q\in\Omega_{r}}c_{Q}\int_{{{\mathbb{R}}}^{2}}dx^{+}\ \tilde{\varphi}_{Q,1}^{n}(x^{+})\tilde{\varphi}_{Q,2}^{n}(x^{+}-a^{+})

\displaystyle=e^{-ic_{r}m_{n}\vec{a}\cdot\tilde{f}_{0}^{n}}\sum_{Q\in\Omega_{r}}c_{Q}\int_{{{\mathbb{R}}}^{2}}dx_{r}^{+}\int_{{{\mathbb{R}}}^{2}}dx_{r^{+}}^{+}\ \tilde{\varphi}_{Q,1}^{n}(x_{r}^{+})\delta(a^{+}+x_{r^{+}}^{+}-x_{r}^{+})\tilde{\varphi}_{Q,2}^{n}(x_{r^{+}}^{+}),

(8.24)

whereby
\delta
is Dirac delta function. Note that the coefficients
c_{Q}
were defined in Equation (
8.2
).

Theorem 8.34

(Clustering Theorem)

Suppose the assumptions in Definition
8.24
hold with
\varphi_{i}
is compactly supported for
i=1,2
.
Assume that
0\neq c_{r}
in Equation (
8.11
).

Recall
m_{0}
is the mass gap of the 4-dimensional quantum Yang-Mills theory, and
\vec{a}=\sum_{b=0}^{3}a^{b}e_{b}
, with
a^{-}=(a^{0},a^{1})
,
a^{+}=(a^{2},a^{3})
. There exist positive constants
\epsilon
,
C
, and
p_{i},q_{i}\in\mathbb{N}
for
i=1,2
, such that

\displaystyle\left|h_{12}\left(\vec{a}\right)\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}c_{1,n}c_{2,n}h_{12}^{n}\left(\vec{a}\right)\right|

\displaystyle\leq C\frac{e^{-m_{0}|a^{+}|}}{|a^{+}|-\epsilon}\parallel\varphi_{1}\parallel_{p_{1},q_{1}}\parallel\varphi_{2}\parallel_{p_{2},q_{2}},

(8.25)

provided
|a^{+}|>\epsilon
, with
\epsilon
depending only on the support of
\varphi_{1}
and
\varphi_{2}
. And,
C=O\left(\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}|c_{1,n}c_{2,n}|C(\rho_{n})^{\tilde{k}}e^{m_{0}\epsilon}\right)
for some fixed integer
\tilde{k}>0
. Note that
a^{+}=(a^{2},a^{3})
are coordinates with respect to
\{e_{2},e_{3}\}
, spanning the
x^{2}-x^{3}
plane
S_{0}
.

Proof.
Just like in the proof of Theorem
8.32
, it suffices to prove it for
\varphi_{1}\in\mathcal{Y}_{\kappa_{r}}
and
\varphi_{2}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{\pi_{s}}
. Recall in Definition
8.28
, we defined a new basis
\{\tilde{f}_{a}^{n}\}_{a=0}^{3}
, which depends on the representation
\rho_{n}
on
\mathfrak{g}
. Let
(a^{0},a^{1},a^{2},a^{3})
be coordinates with respect to the basis
\{\tilde{f}_{a}^{n}\}_{a=0}^{3}
. Further recall that
h_{12}^{n}
is independent of the
a^{1}
coordinate, so we will henceforth write
h_{12}^{n}(\vec{a})=h_{12}^{n}(a^{0},a^{+})
,
a^{+}=(a^{2},a^{3})
.

Define

D(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2})=\left\{\vec{x}_{\tau}-\vec{y}_{\theta}\in{{\mathbb{R}}}^{4}|\ \{\vec{x}_{\tau}\}_{\tau=1}^{r}\in{\rm supp}\ \varphi_{1},\ \{\vec{y}_{\theta}\}_{\theta=r+1}^{r+s}\in{\rm supp}\ \varphi_{2}\right\},

and
consider orthogonally projecting it onto the
x^{2}-x^{3}
plane spanned by
\{e_{2},e_{3}\}
, this projected set denoted by
D^{0}
. It is bounded.

Denote a compact set
D^{1}
, which is the convex closure of
D^{0}
, which lies in the plane spanned by
\{e_{2},e_{3}\}
. Then, we see that there exists an
\epsilon>0
such that
D_{1}
lies in an open disc of radius
\epsilon
, center at the origin.

Let
g
be any compact Schwartz function on
{{\mathbb{R}}}
. Consider the following elliptic equation
3
3

3

We used an elliptic equation, because the total energy squared is then given by
(c_{r}m_{n})^{2}+\sum_{i=1}^{3}q^{i,2}
