, which is mass squared plus total momentum squared. The generator for translation in the time-like direction
\tilde{f}_{0}^{n}
, is given by the mass eigenvalue
-c_{r}m_{n}
, and not energy eigenvalue.

\left(\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial a^{0,2}}-\hat{\mathcal{P}}\right)\Psi_{n}(a^{0},a):=\left(\sum_{i=0}^{3}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial a^{i,2}}\right)\Psi_{n}(a^{0},a)=h_{12}^{n}(a^{0},a^{+})g(a^{1}).

Refer to Notation
8.33
. We leave to the reader to verify from Equation (
8.24
), (
r^{+}\equiv r+1
)

\displaystyle\Psi_{n}

\displaystyle(a^{0},a)=e^{ic_{r}m_{n}a^{0}}\tilde{G}(n;a),\ {\rm and}

\displaystyle\tilde{G}

\displaystyle(n;a)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{Q\in\Omega_{r}}c_{Q}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}d\xi^{1}\int_{{{\mathbb{R}}}^{2\times 2}}dx_{r}^{+}dx_{r^{+}}^{+}\tilde{\varphi}_{Q,1}^{n}(x_{r}^{+})G_{n}(a^{1}-\xi^{1};x_{r}^{+},x_{r^{+}}^{+}+a^{+})\tilde{\varphi}_{Q,2}^{n}(x_{r^{+}}^{+})g(\xi^{1}),

whereby
G_{n}(a^{1}-\xi^{1};x_{r}^{+},x_{r+1}^{+}+a^{+})
is a Green’s function that solves

\displaystyle\left(-(c_{r}m_{n})^{2}+\sum_{i=1}^{3}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial a^{i,2}}\right)G_{n}(a^{1}-\xi^{1};x_{r}^{+},x_{r+1}^{+}+a^{+})

\displaystyle=\delta(a^{1}-\xi^{1},x_{r}^{+}-x_{r+1}^{+}-a^{+})

\displaystyle\equiv\delta(a-\xi_{r}),

whereby
\delta
is the Dirac delta function,
a=(a^{1},a^{+})
and
\xi_{r}=(\xi^{1},\xi_{r}^{+})\equiv(\xi^{1},x_{r}^{+}-x_{r+1}^{+})
.

Write
\omega=|c_{r}m_{n}|\neq 0
and
q=(q^{1},q^{2},q^{3})
be the momentum coordinates dual to
a\equiv(a^{1},a^{2},a^{3})
. Using Fourier Transform techniques, the solution is given by

G_{n}(a^{1}-\xi^{1};x_{r}^{+},x_{r+1}^{+}+a^{+})=-\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{3/2}}\int_{{{\mathbb{R}}}^{3}}dq\ \frac{e^{iq\cdot(a-\xi_{r})}}{\omega^{2}+|q|^{2}}.

Note that the integrand is invariant under spatial rotation. Without any loss of generality, we can assume that
(a-\xi_{r})\equiv(a^{1}-\xi^{1},a^{+}-\xi_{r}^{+})
lies in the
z
-axis. Hence,
q\cdot(a-\xi_{r})=|q|R\cos\theta
, whereby
R=|(a-\xi_{r})|
and
\theta
is the azimuthal angle a vector in 3-dimensional space makes with the positive
z
- axis.

Integrate out the polar and azimuthal angles in spherical coordinates, we have

\displaystyle G_{n}(a^{1}-\xi^{1};x_{r}^{+},x_{r+1}^{+}+a^{+})

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{1/2}}\frac{1}{iR}\int_{0}^{\infty}\lambda\frac{e^{iR\lambda}-e^{-iR\lambda}}{\omega^{2}+\lambda^{2}}\ d\lambda

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{1/2}}\frac{1}{iR}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{\lambda e^{iR\lambda}}{(\lambda-i\omega)(\lambda+i\omega)}\ d\lambda.

We will evaluate this integral using Cauchy’s Residue Theorem, by considering an upper semi-circle, which contains a simple pole at
z=i\omega
in its interior. We choose the upper semi-circle because on the upper half plane,
|e^{iRz}|\leq 1
has an exponential decay. Finally taking its radius to be infinity, the result is that the integral is equal to

G_{n}(a^{1}-\xi^{1};x_{r}^{+},x_{r+1}^{+}+a^{+})=-\frac{2\pi i}{(2\pi)^{1/2}}\frac{1}{iR}\frac{i\omega e^{-R\omega}}{2i\omega}=-\sqrt{2\pi}\frac{e^{-R|c_{r}m_{n}|}}{2R},

with
R^{2}=|a^{1}-\xi^{1}|^{2}+|a^{2}+\xi_{r}^{2}|^{2}+|a^{3}+\xi_{r}^{3}|^{2}
.

Write

\displaystyle K

\displaystyle(a^{+},c_{r}m_{n})

\displaystyle:=\int_{{{\mathbb{R}}}^{2}}dy^{+}\left|\tilde{\varphi}_{Q,1}^{n}(y^{+})\right|\cdot\int_{{{\mathbb{R}}}^{2}}dx^{+}\left|\left[(c_{r}m_{n})^{2}+\hat{\mathcal{P}}\right]\tilde{\varphi}_{Q,2}^{n}(x^{+})\right|\cdot\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{2}}\frac{e^{-m_{0}|a^{+}+x^{+}-y^{+}|}}{|a^{+}+x^{+}-y^{+}|}.

Therefore, (
G_{n}(a-\xi):=G_{n}(a^{1}-\xi^{1};x^{-},x^{+}+a^{+})
)

\displaystyle\left|h_{12}^{n}(\vec{a})g\left(a^{1}\right)\right|=\left|\left[\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial a^{0,2}}-\hat{\mathcal{P}}\right]\Psi_{n}(a^{0},a)\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\sum_{Q\in\Omega_{r}}c_{Q}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}d\xi^{1}\int_{{{\mathbb{R}}}^{4}}d\vec{x}\ \tilde{\varphi}_{Q,1}^{n}(x^{-})G_{n}(a-\xi)\left[(c_{r}m_{n})^{2}+\hat{\mathcal{P}}\right]\tilde{\varphi}_{Q,2}^{n}(x^{+})g(\xi^{1})\right|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{Q\in\Omega_{r}}|c_{Q}|\int_{{{\mathbb{R}}}}\ d\xi^{1}\left[|g(\xi^{1})|+\frac{1}{m_{0}^{2}}|g^{\prime\prime}(\xi^{1})|\right]K(a^{+},c_{r}m_{n})

\displaystyle\leq C(\rho_{n})^{\tilde{k}}e^{m_{0}\epsilon}\parallel g\parallel_{p_{3},q_{3}}\parallel\varphi_{1}\parallel_{p_{1},q_{1}}\parallel\varphi_{2}\parallel_{p_{2},q_{2}}\cdot\frac{e^{-m_{0}|a^{+}|}}{|a^{+}|-\epsilon},

for some natural numbers
\tilde{k}
,
p_{i}
’s,
q_{i}
’s, provided
|x^{+}-y^{+}|\leq\epsilon<|a^{+}|
is large enough. Note that all these natural numbers are independent of
n
,
a^{+}
and the Schwartz functions. We can assume that
C(\rho_{n})\geq 1
, otherwise we will replace it with
1+C(\rho_{n})
in the above inequality.

Because the above inequality holds for any compact Schwartz function
g:{{\mathbb{R}}}\rightarrow{{\mathbb{R}}}
and any
a^{1}\in{{\mathbb{R}}}
, we have the inequality

|h_{12}^{n}(\vec{a})|\leq C(\rho_{n})^{\tilde{k}}e^{m_{0}\epsilon}\parallel\varphi_{1}\parallel_{p_{1},q_{1}}\parallel\varphi_{2}\parallel_{p_{2},q_{2}}\cdot\frac{e^{-m_{0}|a^{+}|}}{|a^{+}|-\epsilon},

for
|a^{+}|>\epsilon
.

Since the above inequality holds for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we see that Equation (
8.25
) follows immediately from triangle inequality and Equation (
8.17
).

Remark 8.35

When the space like separation
\vec{a}
lies in the
x^{2}-x^{3}
plane or
S_{0}
, then
h_{12}(\vec{a})
is equivalent to the vacuum expectation
\left\langle T_{1}^{n}\left(\vec{y}\right)P_{0}T_{2}^{n}\left(\vec{x}\right)1,1\right\rangle
from Definition
8.26
. Suppose we are given two sets of cluster points
\{\vec{x}_{\theta}\in{{\mathbb{R}}}^{4}\}_{\theta=1}^{r}
,
\{\vec{y}_{\tau}\in{{\mathbb{R}}}^{4}\}_{\tau=1}^{s}
, and project them orthogonally onto
S_{0}
, as
A=\{x_{\theta}^{+}\in S_{0}\}_{\theta=1}^{r}
,
B=\{y_{\tau}^{+}\in S_{0}\}_{\tau=1}^{s}
respectively. Clustering Theorem hence says that the vacuum expectation decays exponentially, at a rate dependent on the mass gap
m_{0}
, provided the space-like separation
a^{2}e_{2}+a^{2}e_{3}\in S_{0}
between
A,B\subset S_{0}
, is large enough. In other words,

\displaystyle\left|\mathscr{W}^{n}\left(\{\vec{x}_{\tau}\}_{\tau=1}^{r},\{\vec{x}_{\theta}+\vec{a}\}_{\theta=r^{+}}^{r+s}\right)\right|\leq C(n)e^{-m_{0}|a^{+}|},

for some constant
C(n)
dependent on
n
, and
a^{+}
larger than the space-like separation between the two sets in
S_{0}
.

Note that Equation (
8.22
) holds, even for space-like distance
|a^{+}|
small and close to zero. When there is no minimum mass gap
m_{0}
, the given upper bound is not meaningful for small
|a^{+}|
. For
|a^{+}|
small and for each component mass gap
m_{n}>1
, we see that the heavier the mass gap
m_{n}
, the faster is the rate of decay. Of course, when
|a^{+}|
is large, then the contribution of
m_{0}
to the inverse power decay law is minimal.

An inverse power decay is definitely not as rapid a decline, as an exponential decay, which was proved in
[
22
]
. They showed that the decay is at most
\frac{1}{|a^{+}|^{3/2}}e^{-m_{0}|a^{+}|}
, which is faster than our result. But do note that the authors proved exponential decay, by using a homogeneous wave equation, for the cases when
\varphi_{1}
,
\varphi_{2}
are both compactly supported, and the space-like separation needs to be greater than some
\epsilon
. In contrast, we used an elliptic equation, reason given in the footnote. Any of the three cases in Remark
8.19
will imply that
c_{r}\neq 0
. The rate of decay
\frac{1}{|a^{+}|}e^{-m_{0}|a^{+}|}
which we have shown, is asymptotically the same as the Yukawa potential, who used it as a basis to describe the nuclear force. Refer to
[
9
]
.

For large space-like separation, an exponential decay will decay a lot faster than the inverse power decay given in Equation (
8.22
). But for small space-like separation, an inverse power decay might be better.

Local commutativity in Wightman’s last axiom allows us to define a Lorentz transformation
\Lambda_{n}
in Definition
8.28
, which implies an exponential decay
e^{-m_{n}|a^{+}|}
, but depending on each
n\in\mathbb{N}
. We will need a Yang-Mills path integral, to prove the existence of a minimum positive mass gap
m_{0}\leq m_{n}
, which gives us a best possible exponential decay
e^{-m_{0}|a^{+}|}
, independent of the representation
\rho_{n}
on the simple Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
, for the vacuum expectation.

Appendix A
Surface Integrals

Let
\sigma\equiv(\sigma_{0},\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\sigma_{3}):[0,1]^{2}\equiv I^{2}\rightarrow{{\mathbb{R}}}^{4}
be a parametrization of a surface
S\subset{{\mathbb{R}}}^{4}
. Here,
\sigma^{\prime}=\partial\sigma/\partial s
and
\dot{\sigma}=\partial\sigma/\partial t
.

Definition A.1

For
a,b=0,1,2,3
, define Jacobian matrices,

\displaystyle J_{ab}^{\sigma}(s,t)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\sigma_{a}^{\prime}(s,t)&\dot{\sigma}_{a}(s,t)\\
\sigma_{b}^{\prime}(s,t)&\dot{\sigma}_{b}(s,t)\\
\end{array}\right),\ a\neq b,

and write
|J^{\sigma}_{ab}|=\sqrt{[\det{J^{\sigma}_{ab}}]^{2}}
and
W_{ab}^{cd}:=J_{cd}^{\sigma}J_{ab}^{\sigma,-1}
,
a,b,c,d
all distinct. Note that
W_{cd}^{ab}=(W_{ab}^{cd})^{-1}
.

For
a,b,c,d
all distinct, define
\rho_{\sigma}^{ab}:I^{2}\rightarrow{{\mathbb{R}}}
by

\displaystyle\rho_{\sigma}^{ab}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{\det\left[1+W_{ab}^{cd,T}W_{ab}^{cd}\right]}}\equiv\frac{|J_{ab}^{\sigma}|}{\sqrt{\det\left[J_{ab}^{\sigma,T}J_{ab}^{\sigma}+J_{cd}^{\sigma,T}J_{cd}^{\sigma}\right]}}.

(A.3)

And

\int_{S}d\rho:=\sum_{0\leq a<b\leq 3}\int_{I^{2}}\rho_{\sigma}^{ab}(s,t)|J_{ab}^{\sigma}|(s,t)\ dsdt,

which is independent of the parametrization
\sigma
used.

We leave to the reader to check that
\int_{S}d\rho
gives us the area of the surface
S
. Area of a surface is invariant under spatial rotation, but it is not invariant under boost.

To construct an unitary representation of the Lorentz group, we need to consider imaginary time axis. Instead of using
\rho_{\sigma}^{ab}
as defined in Equation (
A.3
) by some parametrization
\sigma
for
S
, we will replace the time component
\sigma_{0}
with imaginary time
i\sigma_{0}
.

Definition A.2

Let
\sigma:[0,1]^{2}\equiv I^{2}\rightarrow{{\mathbb{R}}}^{4}
be a parametrization of a surface
S\subset{{\mathbb{R}}}^{4}
.
For
a,b=0,1,2,3
and
a<b
, define Jacobian matrices,

\displaystyle\acute{J}_{ab}^{\sigma}(s,t)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\ \ \ \ J_{ab}^{\sigma}(s,t),&\hbox{$a\neq 0$;}\\
\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}i\sigma_{a}^{\prime}(s,t)&i\dot{\sigma}_{a}(s,t)\\
\sigma_{b}^{\prime}(s,t)&\dot{\sigma}_{b}(s,t)\\
\end{array}\right),&\hbox{$a=0$.}\end{array}\right.

For
a,b,c,d
all distinct, define
\acute{\rho}_{\sigma}^{ab}:I^{2}\rightarrow{{\mathbb{C}}}
by

\displaystyle\acute{\rho}_{\sigma}^{ab}:=

\displaystyle\frac{\det\acute{J}_{ab}^{\sigma}}{\sqrt{\det\left[\acute{J}_{ab}^{\sigma,T}\acute{J}_{ab}^{\sigma}+\acute{J}_{cd}^{\sigma,T}\acute{J}_{cd}^{\sigma}\right]}},

and

\displaystyle\int_{S}\ d\acute{\rho}:=

\displaystyle\sum_{0\leq a<b\leq 3}\int_{I^{2}}\acute{\rho}_{\sigma}^{ab}(s,t)[\det\acute{J}_{ab}^{\sigma}(\hat{s})]\ d\hat{s},

\displaystyle\int_{S}d|\acute{\rho}|:=

\displaystyle\int_{I^{2}}\left|\sum_{0\leq a<b\leq 3}\acute{\rho}_{\sigma}^{ab}(\hat{s})[\det\acute{J}_{ab}^{\sigma}](\hat{s})\right|\ d\hat{s}.

We will also write

\displaystyle\acute{\rho}_{\sigma}(\hat{s})=

\displaystyle\sum_{0\leq a<b\leq 3}\acute{\rho}_{\sigma}^{ab}(\hat{s})[\det\acute{J}_{ab}^{\sigma}](\hat{s}),

\displaystyle|\acute{\rho}_{\sigma}|(\hat{s})=

\displaystyle\left|\sum_{0\leq a<b\leq 3}\acute{\rho}_{\sigma}^{ab}(\hat{s})[\det\acute{J}_{ab}^{\sigma}](\hat{s})\right|.

Lemma A.3

Let
S
be a compact time-like or space-like surface, contained in a plane. Then
\int_{S}d\acute{\rho}
and
\int_{S}d|\acute{\rho}|
remain invariant under any Lorentz transformation
\Lambda:S\mapsto\hat{S}=\Lambda S
,
\Lambda
is a
4\times 4
Lorentz matrix.

Proof.
Let
\sigma:I^{2}\rightarrow S
be a parametrization of
S
. Then
\hat{\sigma}=\Lambda\sigma
is a parametrization of
\hat{S}=\Lambda S
. We leave to the reader to verify that

\sum_{0\leq a<b\leq 3}\acute{\rho}_{\hat{\sigma}}^{ab}[\det\acute{J}_{ab}^{\hat{\sigma}}]=\sqrt{[\sigma^{\prime}\cdot\sigma^{\prime}][\dot{\sigma}\cdot\dot{\sigma}]-[\sigma^{\prime}\cdot\dot{\sigma}]^{2}}.

Since
\vec{x}\cdot\vec{y}
is invariant under Lorentz transformation, we thus have

\displaystyle\sum_{0\leq a<b\leq 3}\acute{\rho}_{\hat{\sigma}}^{ab}[\det\acute{J}_{ab}^{\hat{\sigma}}]

\displaystyle=\sum_{0\leq a<b\leq 3}\acute{\rho}_{\sigma}^{ab}[\det\acute{J}_{ab}^{\sigma}].

This shows that
\int_{\hat{S}}d\acute{\rho}=\int_{S}d\acute{\rho}
and
\int_{\hat{S}}d|\acute{\rho}|=\int_{S}d|\acute{\rho}|
.

Remark A.4

1.

When
S
is a surface in spatial
{{\mathbb{R}}}^{3}
, we see that
\int_{S}d|\acute{\rho}|=\int_{S}d\rho
, which is the area of the surface
S
.

2.

Note that
\int_{S}d\acute{\rho}
is complex valued.

3.

As a consequence, when
S
is a space-like surface,
\int_{S}d\acute{\rho}
is real; when
S
is a time-like surface,
\int_{S}d\acute{\rho}
is purely imaginary. That is,
\int_{S}d\acute{\rho}=i\int_{S}d|\acute{\rho}|
.

4.

By definition and from the proof, we see that
\acute{\rho}_{\sigma}=\acute{\rho}_{\Lambda\sigma+\vec{a}}
, for any Lorentz transformation
\Lambda
and any vector
\vec{a}
.

Appendix B
Lorentz transformation of a space-like vector

Lemma B.1

Let
S
be a space-like rectangular surface contained in a plane. Without loss of generality, we assume a parametrization of
S
given in Definition
2.2
.
From Definition
2.5
, we can define a basis
\{\hat{f}_{a}\}_{a=0}^{3}
.

Let
0\neq\vec{v}
be a space-like directional vector lying in the time-like plane
\vec{x}+S^{\flat}
, i.e.
\vec{v}=s\hat{f}_{0}+\bar{s}\hat{f}_{1}\in S^{\flat}
, whereby
|\bar{s}|>|s|
, and
\vec{x}\in S
.
There exists a finite sequence of translations and Lorentz transformations, which depends on
\vec{x},\vec{v}
, such that
\vec{v}\in\vec{x}+S^{\flat}\mapsto-\vec{v}\in\vec{x}+S^{\flat}
.

Proof.
First, assume that
S
contains the zero vector. Without any loss of generality, we assume that
S
is parametrized according to Definition
2.2
, with
\vec{a}=0
. From this parametrization, we can define
\{\hat{f}_{a}\}_{a=0}^{3}
as in Definition
2.5
, and it is easy to see that we have a Lorentz transformation
\bar{\Lambda}
which sends
\bar{\Lambda}:\hat{f}_{a}\mapsto e_{a}
, for
a=0,\cdots,3
. Hence,
\bar{\Lambda}S\subseteq S_{0}
.

Then,
\bar{\Lambda}\vec{x}=\sum_{a=0}^{3}x^{a}e_{a}=x^{2}e_{2}+x^{3}e_{3}
.
Let
R_{i}
be a rotation about
x^{i}
-axis, and
\Lambda
be boost in the
e_{1}
direction, that sends

\Lambda:se_{0}+\bar{s}e_{1}\mapsto\left[{\rm sgn}(\bar{s})\sqrt{\bar{s}^{2}-s^{2}}\right]e_{1},

whereby
{\rm sgn}(\bar{s})
is the sign of
\bar{s}
.

Then, we have

\displaystyle\vec{x}

\displaystyle+\vec{v}=s\hat{f}_{0}+\bar{s}\hat{f}_{1}+x^{2}\hat{f}_{2}+x^{3}\hat{f}_{3}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow_{\bar{\Lambda}}(s,\bar{s},x^{2},x^{3})\equiv se_{0}+\bar{s}e_{1}+x^{2}e_{2}+x^{3}e_{3}\longrightarrow_{\Lambda}(0,{\rm sgn}(\bar{s})\sqrt{\bar{s}^{2}-s^{2}},x^{2},x^{3})

\displaystyle\longrightarrow_{R_{3}}(0,{\rm sgn}(\bar{s})\sqrt{x^{2,2}+\bar{s}^{2}-s^{2}},0,x^{3})\longrightarrow_{R_{2}}-(0,{\rm sgn}(\bar{s})\sqrt{x^{2,2}+\bar{s}^{2}-s^{2}},0,x^{3})

\displaystyle\longrightarrow_{R_{3}^{-1}}-(0,{\rm sgn}(\bar{s})\sqrt{\bar{s}^{2}-s^{2}},x^{2},x^{3})\longrightarrow_{\Lambda^{-1}}-(s,\bar{s},x^{2},x^{3})

\displaystyle\longrightarrow_{R_{1}}(-s,-\bar{s},x^{2},x^{3})\longrightarrow_{\bar{\Lambda}^{-1}}-s\hat{f}_{0}-\bar{s}\hat{f}_{1}+x^{2}\hat{f}_{2}+x^{3}\hat{f}_{3}.

Hence, we have

\tilde{\Lambda}(\vec{x},\vec{v}):=\bar{\Lambda}^{-1}R_{1}\Lambda^{-1}R_{3}^{-1}R_{2}R_{3}\Lambda\bar{\Lambda},

which sends
\vec{v}\in\vec{x}+S^{\flat}\mapsto-\vec{v}\in\vec{x}+S^{\flat}
. Note that all the rotations depend on
\vec{x}
and
\vec{v}
, except
R_{1}
and
R_{2}
, which rotate by angle
\pi
radians.

Suppose
\tilde{S}
does not contain the origin. Then, we consider for
\vec{a}\in\tilde{S}
,
S=U(-\vec{a},1)\tilde{S}
, which contains the origin. For any space-like vector
\vec{v}\in\vec{x}-\vec{a}+S^{\flat}
, we can map
\vec{v}\mapsto-\vec{v}
, using the above transformations. Finally, apply
U(\vec{a},1)
to translate back to
\tilde{S}
.

Remark B.2

On the
x^{2}-x^{3}
plane or
S_{0}
, any two points on it are space-like separated. Clearly, there is a Lorentz transformation (rotation) which transforms
\vec{p}\in S_{0}\mapsto-\vec{p}\in S_{0}
. In general, we see that there is a sequence of translations and Lorentz transformations which transforms
\vec{p}\in S\mapsto-\vec{p}\in S
, for some space-like plane
S
.

On a time-like plane
S
, we do not have a rotation in the Lorentz group that maps a time-like vector
\vec{p}\in S\mapsto-\vec{p}\in S
. It is not possible to do time and space inversion simultaneously for a time-like vector using Lorentz transformations.

Lemma B.3

Refer to Definition
2.5
. Let
S^{\flat}
be a time-like plane spanned by
\{\hat{f}_{0},\hat{f}_{1}\}
. Fix a time-like vector
u\in S^{\flat}
. For any time-like or space-like vector
v\in S^{\flat}
, there exist a
\phi
,
\theta
depending on
u
and
v
respectively, such that

\displaystyle v\cdot u=

\displaystyle\sinh(\phi-\theta)=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\cosh(\phi-\theta)\\
\sinh(\phi-\theta)\\
\end{array}\right)\cdot\left(\begin{array}[]{c}0\\
1\\
\end{array}\right),

when
v
is space-like; and

\displaystyle v\cdot u=

\displaystyle-\cosh(\phi-\theta)=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\cosh(\phi-\theta)\\
\sinh(\phi-\theta)\\
\end{array}\right)\cdot\left(\begin{array}[]{c}1\\
0\\
\end{array}\right),

when
v
is time-like.

Proof.
Any vector on
S^{\flat}
can be represented as a 2-vector
(a,b)^{T}
, which are the coordinates with respect to the basis
\{\hat{f}_{0},\hat{f}_{1}\}
. Define

\Lambda(\theta)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\cosh\theta&\ \sinh\theta\\
\sinh\theta&\ \cosh\theta\\
\end{array}\right).

(B.1)

For a time-like vector, we will write it as
\Lambda(\phi)(1,0)^{T}
.
For any space-like vector, we will write it as
\Lambda(\theta)(0,1)^{T}
. Then, we have

\displaystyle\Lambda(\theta)\left(\begin{array}[]{c}0\\
1\\
\end{array}\right)\cdot\Lambda(\phi)\left(\begin{array}[]{c}1\\
0\\
\end{array}\right)=

\displaystyle\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\sinh\theta\\
\cosh\theta\\
\end{array}\right)\cdot\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\cosh\phi\\
\sinh\phi\\
\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\sinh\theta\cosh\phi+\cosh\theta\sinh\phi=\sinh(\phi-\theta)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\cosh(\phi-\theta)\\
\sinh(\phi-\theta)\\
\end{array}\right)\cdot\left(\begin{array}[]{c}0\\
1\\
\end{array}\right).

A similar calculation will show that

\Lambda(\theta)\left(\begin{array}[]{c}1\\
0\\
\end{array}\right)\cdot\Lambda(\phi)\left(\begin{array}[]{c}1\\
0\\
\end{array}\right)=-\cosh(\theta-\phi)=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\cosh(\phi-\theta)\\
\sinh(\phi-\theta)\\
\end{array}\right)\cdot\left(\begin{array}[]{c}1\\
0\\
\end{array}\right).

From the calculations, when
\theta=\phi
, we see that
e^{-iu\cdot v}=\cos(0)=1
, which is real. This happens when
v
is space-like,
u
is time-like. However, if both are time-like or both are space-like, then we have
e^{-iu\cdot v}=\cos(u\cdot v)-i\sin(u\cdot v)\neq 1
, unless
u\cdot v=n\pi
for
n\in\mathbb{Z}
.
